# Chapter 3

*   Astha Bhanot  
    Princess Nourah Bint Abdulrahman University, Saudi Arabia

ABSTRACT

This chapter’s goal is to assess blockchain technology's potential, current condition, and how some aspects of this revolutionary creation could change how “business as usual” is done across various industries. This study unifies the theoretical background of a profusion of research articles published in highly regarded scientific journals over the past 10 years, as well as some findings, in order to streamline the review and cover the rapidly growing blockchain domain. The researcher suggests a thorough classification of blockchain-enabled applications covering a range of industries, including supply chain, business, health, IoT, privacy, data management, and others based on a structured, systematic review and thematic content analysis of the current literature. It also draws attention to important themes, patterns, and fresh study opportunities along with the defects in the previously disclosed information, namely the limits of the blockchain technology and how these constraints affect businesses as well as other sectors of the economy.

INTRODUCTION

Bitcoin's mysterious creator, Satoshi Nakamoto, proposed the blockchain technology, a distributed peer-to-peer linked-structure, about a decade ago to address the issues of double spending and transaction order (Nakamoto, 2008). Bitcoin arranges transactions and collects them into blocks of a fixed size and a common timestamp. Each block in a blockchain includes a hash of the prior block, and it is the job of the network's nodes to ensure that the blocks are linked in chronological order. Since all transactions are recorded in this immutable ledger, it can be verified by any interested party.

Since apps and transactions that previously require centralized architectures or trusted third parties to verify them can now operate in a decentralized manner with the same level of confidence, blockchains have caused significant disruptions to established business operations. Transparency, resilience, auditability, and security are just some of the benefits you'll enjoy thanks to blockchain's built-in qualities. A blockchain is similar to a distributed database, except that the committed blocks are permanent and appear as a list in chronological order. It's easy to see how this would be perfect for the financial industry, as institutions like banks could work together under a single blockchain to expedite their clients' transactions. As a result, blockchain enables auditing of transactions, which provides an additional layer of transparency. Businesses are investing in this technology because they believe it will allow them to create decentralized architectures with reduced transaction costs and improved security and transparency (Bracamonte, 2017).

For this reason, blockchains are more than just a passing fad. The significance of blockchain is demonstrated by the fact that there are already more than 1900 different cryptocurrencies. Due to the diversity of bitcoin use cases, rapid expansion like this could soon lead to incompatibilities. blockchain technology is finding applications beyond cryptocurrency, with Smart Contracts playing a pivotal part in this expansion. Therefore, a SC is an agreement between parties in which the conditions are automatically enforced even while the parties do not trust each other. In this sense, SCs are blockchain-specific scripts that are executed and stored in the blockchain without requiring a central authority. More complicated processes and interactions are made possible by blockchain-based systems that support SCs, thus establishing a new paradigm with practically infinite uses.

Therefore, the importance of blockchain technology is rising. Nearly a third of C-suite executives report either contemplating or actively use blockchain technology (IBM, 2017). Researchers and developers are already familiar with the new technology's potential and are investigating its uses in a wide variety of fields. There are three distinct generations of blockchains, all defined by their target demographic. There is Blockchain 1.0, which includes applications allowing digital cryptocurrency transactions; Blockchain 2.0, which includes SCs and a set of applications extending beyond cryptocurrency transactions; and Blockchain 3.0, which includes applications in areas such as government, health, science, and the Internet of Things ([Brandão et al. 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch003.xhtml#r2)).

The researcher claim that the state-of-the-art of blockchain-enabled applications has insufficient attention, despite the fact that there are many evaluations of blockchain technology. The breadth and depth of blockchains' potential uses are underexplored. There are certain assessments that zero in on the specific function of blockchain, such as the creation of distributed and data-heavy Internet of Things applications and the decentralized administration of massive amounts of data. Other analyses consider how the blockchain can facilitate trust and decentralization in service systems and P2P networks, as well as its possible security flaws. Additionally, several polls have concentrated on the blockchain's security and privacy features in relation to monetary transactions. The fundamental motivation for this study was the obvious deficiency in the existing literature of a comprehensive and systematic overview of the state-of-the-art blockchain-enabled apps already in use.

OVERVIEW OF BLOCKCHAIN

A blockchain can be thought of as a decentralised, append-only, timestamped data model in concept. Blockchain technology enables a decentralised, peer-to-peer network in which users don't have to rely on a central authority to conduct their interactions in a transparent manner. To this end, one might think of blockchain as a network of related mechanisms ([Figure 1](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch003.xhtml#f01)), each of which contributes to the overall functionality of the system. Transactions between peers that have been digitally signed are the foundation of this system. The term “transaction” refers to any kind of deal between two parties, whether it's the exchange of money or goods, the fulfilment of a certain task, etc. This deal is sealed by at least one party and broadcast to the surrounding areas. Any participant in a blockchain network is referred to as a “node.” Full nodes, on the other hand, are those that check every single rule in the blockchain. These nodes collect payments into blocks and decide which ones are legitimate and should be recorded on the blockchain and which ones should be discarded. If Bob receives one bitcoin from Alice, then the transaction is called genuine. Though Alice may have attempted a transfer of give bitcoin, as a resource, to Carol. Therefore, nodes must come to terms on which transaction must be maintained in the blockchain to ensure there are no tainted forks or splits.

The aim of the second Consensus tier is just this. Consensus mechanisms vary from one blockchain design to the next. Proof-of-work is the most well-known example. To provide authenticity and verifiability, Proof-of-Work (PoW) needs the solution of a complex computing procedure, such as discovering hashes with specified patterns, such as a leading number of zeros. Proof-of-Stake (PoS) protocols divide stake blocks in accordance with each miner's current wealth rather than their comparable hash rates. As a result, the selection process is more level-headed and the wealthiest member of the group doesn't end up with all the power. As a result of the huge reduction in power usage and greater scalability, several blockchains, including Ethereum, are gradually transitioning to PoS. The Byzantine Fault Tolerance (BFT) family of protocols is another example of a consensus-based method. In order for blockchains to expand their capabilities, an extra layer called the Compute Interface must be implemented. In practical terms, a blockchain preserves a state consisting of, say, all the transactions that have been made by the users, allowing the balances of all those users to be determined. For increasingly complicated applications, however, it is necessary to maintain state information that is dynamically updated via distributed computing; for example, states that change from one to another when certain conditions are met. Because of this need, smart contracts (SCs) have emerged, which rely on blockchain nodes to carry out their terms. At last, the Governance layer expands the blockchain design to include offline human interactions. Even though blockchain protocols are clearly defined, they are also influenced by the efforts of many people who work together to include novel techniques, enhance existing ones, and fix bugs. These pieces are crucial to the development of each blockchain, yet they occur outside of the blockchain itself. Thus, blockchain governance addresses how these various parties collaborate to generate, maintain, or alter the blockchain's constituent parts. There are a few different types of blockchain networks described in the current literature. Public, private, and federated networks are distinguished by their level of accessibility and management. Anyone can become a user or a node miner in a public blockchain. In addition, all users can carry out actions like making a purchase or entering into a contract. Private blockchains, which along with federated blockchains make up the permissioned blockchain category, typically have a whitelist of authorised users who have access to the network with specific privileges and data access controls. Private blockchain networks can forego costly PoW techniques due to the low likelihood of Sybil attacks. Instead, a plethora of disincentive-based consensus mechanisms might be put into place. Together, formal and informal blockchains form a federated blockchain. The fundamental difference between public blockchain and private blockchain is that public blockchain uses a group of nodes called leader nodes to validate transactions rather than a central authority. This allows for a semi-decentralized architecture in which authority nodes can delegate authority to other nodes. In this article, we provide a more granular blockchain network classification than the current state-of-the-art does by taking into account features like transaction approval time or security aspects like anonymity in addition to more traditional ones like management and ownership of the knowledge stored in the blockchain. Bitcoin, Ethereum, Litecoin, and the vast majority of cryptocurrencies are all examples of popular public blockchain implementations. There is no need to invest in costly infrastructure because the network can look after itself, cutting down on administrative costs significantly. Database administration, auditing, and other performance-intensive solutions are the primary uses for private blockchains. One such open platform for designing and implementing private blockchains is Multichain. In conclusion, federated blockchains see the most widespread application in the financial and manufacturing sectors. The Hyperledger project is one such initiative, as it creates a blockchain architecture that can be used in a variety of different industries. Ethereum has also recently offered instruments for constructing federated blockchains.

LITERATURE REVIEW

Blockchain technology serves as bitcoin's theoretical foundation. Hopes for this technology have been aroused by the fact that all transactions are decentralized and carried out without the assistance of a trustworthy third party. The financial sector today hosts the majority of blockchain-based practical commercial applications, despite the fact that many research and development (R&D) projects are being carried out by firms and organizations with the goal of expanding the use cases for blockchain technology. In this post, we conduct a thorough investigation to pinpoint the industries where blockchain technology services and applications are being created in both the public and private sectors. The findings reveal both reservations about blockchain technology and the potential disruption that it may bring about in a innumerable business sectors.

Brereton et al. (2007) method for conducting a systematic literature review based on existing evidence was used for this investigation. There are a total of 10 steps in the process model, which may be broken down into three distinct time intervals. Recent reviews of blockchain research have shown that the vast bulk of academic attention has been paid to the enhancement and problems with existing blockchain protocols, particularly as they pertain to Bitcoin and other cryptocurrencies. Little is on research that looks into the supposed disruptive potential of blockchain (Glaser & Bezzenberger, 2015; Tapscott & Tapscott, 2006; Yli-Huumo et al., 2016). While there is extensive research in some areas, such as cryptocurrency and payments, there is a general lack of knowledge on how these technologies might be put to practical use ([Risius & Spohrer, 2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch003.xhtml#r20)).

While Brando et al. (2018) mostly looked at Bitcoin and cryptocurrency literature, this work specifically avoided pure cryptocurrency systems and instead investigated general-purpose blockchain use cases. While there have been reviews of blockchain applications, [Seebacher and Schüritz (2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch003.xhtml#r22)) focuses on the ways in which blockchain's unique properties are changing the face of service delivery. [Risius and Spohrer (2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch003.xhtml#r20)) presents a conceptual framework for blockchain research adopted from renowned social media research agenda. It encourages multidisciplinary approaches to blockchain research by displaying a nexus of user and blockchain developer activities across multiple levels of analysis. However, this study proposes and uses a component-based paradigm with a total different focus, one that is oriented at cross-sector analysis for system design and implementations. Financial and cryptocurrency (Kaushik et al., 2017), governance, education, energy, the Internet of Things (IoT), healthcare, and many others are just a few examples of related work that centre on specific domains with varying levels of depth and may or may not adopt a systematic literature review approach ([Zhang et al., 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch003.xhtml#r25)).

Dwivedi et al. (2021) discussed regarding the Industry 4.0 links cutting-edge technologies like blockchain, cloud computing, IoT, MI and AI to increase automation in industrial processes and to create a cyber-physical that integrates the digital and physical worlds. In spite of many difficulties faced in IoT system, including decentralization, security and privacy vulnerabilities, single points for overcoming the difficulties of IoT systems can be handled through blockchain and turn the IoT devices intrinsic features turn the industrial sector into a smart industry (also known as IIoT). The most recent study is divided into three categories: big data and cloud computing (financial and data audits), data storage and management, and industrial sectors (supply chain, energy, and healthcare sector). This article includes interesting discussions based on the various categories like IoT and IIoT along with focus on the intersection between blockchain and IoT using cutting-edge research.

According to Cheng (2022), the need for cross-border electronic data forensics grows, sovereignty and international network security has become a huge task to manage. The countries needs to find solution to these problems in order to advance the process of global cyber-space governance, find international consensus, and achieve win-win cooperation. Based on the aforementioned status quo, a blockchain-based secure e-commerce system is explored and implemented. A secure transaction application system should be developed using the distributed architecture of the blockchain system and the automated execution feature of smart contracts in order to achieve decentralization and address the problem of unreliable intermediaries. In order to ensure data security, data storage is constructed on a system that integrates offchain databases with blockchains, and the blockchain's restriction to read-only and add-only operations prohibits system manipulation. The comprehensive presentation of the system's design and development process is a crucial point of reference for the application of blockchain technology in e-commerce.

[Garcia-Garcia et al. (2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch003.xhtml#r15)) highlighted the rise of BlockChain Technology (BCT), which promises a real revolution in organizational, management, and commercial practices related to the usage of cutting-edge software systems, has been significant. In actuality, BCT promotes a decentralized architecture for process management as well as cooperative work across entities when these entities are involved in a business process. This study aims to discover recommendations for improving business process management step since BCT may be helpful in this management. It also provides a complete literature review in the area of collaborative business processes (CBP) in the BCT industry in order to pinpoint opportunities and gaps for further research. According to this study, the potential that BCT offers for improving CBP administration in a decentralized way are attracting interest from the scientific community, government organizations, and the software industry. Although the field is still young, there are a number of very interesting research directions and important open questions, yet the process of verifying the numerous studies still lacks scientific rigor.

Stallone et al. (2021) aimed to investigate the applications of Blockchain Technology (BCT) in marketing through an accurate procedure of locating, selecting, and analyzing existing companies using BCT in marketing. This is due to the emerging nature of integrating BCT into several business fields concerning the interaction between companies and their customers. The researches have done a qualitative research by taken from web-scraping techniques with a sample of 800 businesses. The data set was divided into five categories of marketing technology in order for thorough analysis. In terms of actual numbers, advertising and e-commerce surpassed the other disciplines of social & connection, content & experience, and data, illuminating practitioners' previous priorities as well as unmet needs for the future. The authors offered future paths for researchers and the creation of tools to methodically produce information and enhance the use of BCT and the work of marketing practitioners.

RESEARCH METHODOLOGY AND EXPLANATION OF THE STUDY

During January 2021, we conducted a comprehensive literature search with no constraints on time period, and in April 2021, we updated the results to include new research. Scopus was the primary academic database utilized to search for occurrences of the word “blockchain” in article titles. Related article citations were also used to conduct supplementary literature searches. Electronic searches also uncovered relevant “grey literature,” or unpublished studies funded by governments or private/public organizations ([Risius & Spohrer, 2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch003.xhtml#r20)). We looked at the top two hundred results on Google to find the published grey literature. The search process involved the usage of synonyms for “blockchain” and “application.” Additional grey literature, such as research as well as panel reports or policy briefs from corporate and public sector institutions/organizations, was discovered through the manual search of reference lists in various papers. There was also heavy reliance on Scopus's various filtering tools. When only the summary of a study was available, we read the entire thing and made a judgment based on it. We have gathered the complete texts of all articles that might be relevant (Glaser & Bezzenberger, 2015). A qualitative analysis software (MAXQDA11) was used to enter all articles and reports that met the inclusion requirements, and the data was then analyzed in terms of emerging themes. The theme content analysis was performed by the reviewers on their own. After comparing and agreeing upon the coded parts across all articles, a unified collection of themes and subthemes emerged. During the year 2021, there was a huge uptick in the amount of books released. By 2021, there will have been about 190 publications about blockchain-enabled apps, up from slightly over 60 in 2020. As a result, the study of blockchain-enabled applications has picked up speed during the past couple of years. This rising trend illustrates the novelty and potential of blockchain technology, as well as the increasing attention it is receiving from the academic community. Although Bitcoin was the initial use of blockchains, it took a few years for the academic community to fully recognize blockchain's potential and take advantage of its various applications. It's no surprise that in the early days of blockchain, Bitcoin was associated with the term, and that in principle, academics were more concerned with building out the underlying infrastructure than finding practical uses for the technology. As a result, there has been an uptick in the publication of scholarly journals covering topics like blockchain technology and its applications beginning in the year 2020.

BLOCKCHAIN IN BUSINESS

Financial services, asset settlement, prediction markets, and monetary transactions are just some of the areas where blockchain technology is now being implemented. The widespread adoption of blockchain technology is seen as crucial to the long-term health of the world economy, which will in turn benefit customers, the current financial system, and society at large.

Financial assets like securities, fiat currency, and derivative contracts are being considered for use in blockchain-enabled applications by the international financial system. Financial transactions, such as those involving stocks and derivatives, digital payments, loan management schemes, general banking services, financial auditing, and the payment and exchange of cryptocurrencies, can all be performed more efficiently thanks to blockchain technology. Several of the largest financial institutions in the world have collaborated to create a blockchain-based infrastructure for the financial sector. Santander, Bank of America, and UniCredit are just some of the banks that make up the Global Payments Steering Group, another example of banks working together. Global Payments System Gold (GPSG) is powered by XRP, a cryptocurrency developed by Ripple that uses a scalable, open-source system to facilitate international transactions (Briner & Denver, 2012).

It's also worth keeping an eye on the developing field of prediction marketplace systems (PMS), which work as oracles or information sources and could have implications for traditional economies and digital currencies. Open source cryptocurrencies that use the blockchain and the Scrypt hashing algorithm to implement PMS in a peer-to-peer fashion are available. A form of Proof-of-Work that allows for substantially quicker transactions than Bitcoin is a decentralized PMS that facilitates pre-event share trading using the “wisdom of the crowds” as a guiding principle ([Center, 2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch003.xhtml#r12)). Users earn points when their predictions of upcoming events in the real world come true. Market items like gold, oil, gas, and currencies could yield interest for token holders. BitShares 2.0 is an upgrade to the original BitShares protocol that provides a suite of decentralized blockchain-based financial services, such as currency exchange and banking operations. The Nasdaq-Citi platform facilitates features like relationship management and investing for non-public businesses. Using the Counterparty protocol, which incorporates financial products as SCs, Ventures (2014) leverages the blockchain 2.0 platform to build a new kind of stock market. Also, there's Coinsetter, a Bitcoin Forex trading platform established in New York City. By providing a framework for SCs to be used in financial transactions and for the creation of incentive structures for globally persistent data services, Plasma is an example of a distributed ledger technology. Commercial property and liability insurance claims processing, contingent convertible bonds, computerized adherence, proxy voting, asset securitization, and the over-the-counter market are all possible examples of other finance-related fields. Savings can be made in areas including central finance reporting, regulation, centralized operations, as well as business operations as a result of blockchain use in the financial sector ([Bruce, 2013](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch003.xhtml#r4); [Burrell, 2016](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch003.xhtml#r5)).

RELIABILITY OF BLOCKCHAIN

Integrity verification is one of the most promising new uses of blockchain technology. Information and transactions relating to the development and maintenance of a product or service can be stored and verified by a blockchain application. IP protection blockchain applications are a subset of the integrity verification blockchain applications. Since “digital art” can relate to intellectual property in general and not simply to online artworks, it follows that blockchain technology might be thought of as all-encompassing in this context. Mediachain aims to use the blockchain to store metadata in order to facilitate media recovery and searching, while Ascribe employs it to shift ownership as well as loan digital assets. Methods of monetization such as Monegraph enable the dissemination of licensed or brand-sponsored content such as online broadcasts, video clips, image reels, as well as more to be monetized and the proceeds shared across the media distribution value chain. When it comes to securing and verifying digital assets, Factom is yet another blockchain-based option. SilentNotary is a blockchain-based service that verifies the authenticity of digitally recorded events, like messages sent over instant messaging apps, photographs, and videos sent via email. The kodakOne platform, which houses the works of registered photographers, accepts the unique payment method Kodakcoin for the purchase of photo licenses and image rights. In recent months, the insurance industry has been paying more and more attention to blockchain technology in many areas, such as sales, underwriting, customer on boarding, claims processing, payments, asset transfers, as well as reinsurance. European insurers, for example, have just created B3i, a blockchain industry effort, to research the technology's potential applications across the insurance industry and speed up the process of standardizing protocols and procedures for widespread adoption. Blockchain-enabled SCs automate a number of insurance industry operations, which leads to significant cost savings, greater efficiency, and faster turnaround times. Possible applications of blockchain technology in the health insurance industry include creating less arbitrary, more up-to-date risk pooling; establishing alerts to take prescribed medications or make regular doctor visits or diagnostic tests; facilitating continuous underwriting as well as pricing assessments; and enabling for more customization as well as individualized coverage.

BLOCKCHAIN IN GOVERNANCE

Over the course of many decades, governments have been tasked with the responsibility of preserving and storing official documents of residents and businesses alike. By eliminating the need for a middleman in financial and administrative processes, applications built on the blockchain have the potential to revolutionize how local and state governments function. Blockchain has the potential to reduce corruption and improve the efficiency of government services due to its immutability, automation, and security in the management of public documents. Particularly, blockchain has the potential to act as a safe communication platform for connecting smart city's various technical, social, and commercial structures. Blockchain governance seeks to decentralize and streamline the provision of services traditionally provided by the state as well as its related public entities without compromising on their legality. Services in this category include, but are not limited to, registering a business or individual, having documents notarized, and obtaining official identity, entering into a marriage contract, paying taxes, and casting a ballot. An instance of a centralized passports service that can be used to identify people all across the world is the World Citizen project. Public services that potentially benefit from the use of blockchains include marriage registrations, patent maintenance, and taxes. Mechanisms capable of reliably determining who the users are and certifying their basic qualities like name, address, credit record, and other personal traits are necessary for the widespread adoption of digital technology. As a result, establishing a reliable digital identity is now essential. Somewhere around a sixth of the world's population has no official identification. Immigrants and refugees are impacted by this since their home nations may refuse to provide the necessary documentation, for example because they are members of the opposition. Thus, blockchain becomes a tool to strengthen global citizens' equality and possibilities. The Internet of Agreements paves the way for the next generation of digital business by creating a link between online material and offline agreements, contracts, and regulations. Due to the rise in electronic shopping, such as e-commerce, the number of disputes has increased, necessitating the development of blockchain apps that use SCs to verify various operations, such as the ownership of specific assets. Since online disagreements are so common, it's crucial that effective conflict management be made available, one that can get beyond barriers of distance and bureaucracy.

PUBLIC SERVICE AND BLOCKCHAIN

We believe that virtual notary, proof of existence, proof of operation, proof of intent, reputation management, as well as dispute resolution are all examples of public services that may be provided to the public without the involvement of government agencies. Keep in mind that PoE, PoO, and PoI are inextricably intertwined and may be independently verified in a blockchain. Governments around the world are exploring the potential benefits of implementing blockchain technology there in public sector, with an eye toward cutting costs and red tape while simultaneously boosting productivity and the authenticity of a wide variety of persistent documents thanks to the secure, decentralised, open, and low-cost database technology. Document authentication, electronic residency strategies, more secure regulatory compliance frameworks, land administration, and taxation reform are all possible public sector uses for blockchain technology.

E-voting has been looked at as a potentially game-changing innovation that might strengthen democracies in the long run by streamlining election processes, lowering associated costs, and increasing voter turnout. Unfortunately, the current generation of electronic voting systems is reliant on proprietary as well as centralized construction by a single company, both of which undermine voters' faith and confidence in the system. Systems of distributed voting, such as these, provide frameworks for enforcing decisions made by a wide range of people. In a futarchy, voters submit not only issues but also potential means of resolving them. To be more specific, people only back policies if they believe the prediction and betting markets would maximise their overall profits. In principle, blockchain technology provides a decentralised, open-source, independently verifiable network that is compliant with domestic legislation and inspires the confidence needed by voters as well as election administrators.

The vast majority of the world's data (90%+) has been generated within the past two years. Due to, among other things, Though both blockchain and Internet of Things technologies have enormous growth potential on their own, their mutually beneficial interaction opens up even more opportunities. Despite their flaws, distributed wireless sensors are a cornerstone of technological and human progress, and they show how blockchain architecture could improve the Internet of Things by mitigating its weaknesses while amplifying its strengths. Block chain and its intrinsic capabilities are largely responsible for the growing interest in and investment in the implementation of decentralised IoT platforms. The goal is to facilitate safe and traceable information flow between various types of smart devices in various contexts. The network's great scalability as well as efficient management are made possible by its automated and decentralised operation. Enhancing brick-and-mortar stores, online marketplaces, and private and public transport systems, blockchain interoperability provides decentralised, secure, instantaneous payment services. Filecoin, a provider of memory storage, and EtherAPIs, which facilitates the monetisation of API calls, are only two examples of apps that aggregate these features. There is potential for similar ideas to be applied to the smartgrid domain to enable the sale of electricity, and in the future, Internet of Things devices might be strongly connected with their cryptographic protocol bank account to provide micro transactions in exchange for services. Distributed networks of Sensors data enable the automatic processing of products in a wide range of situations, including food chains, transport services, and inventory management, all of which are relevant to the concept of provenance and supply chains. Here, the data collected by the devices might be entered into the blockchain either as smart contracts or as regular transactions. A peer-to-peer (P2P) distributed Internet of Things system is illustrated in. A number of problems, such as the high cost of maintenance for centralised systems, might be alleviated with the introduction of blockchain-based IoT solutions. Increased control of IoY systems for maintaining system updates is also possible with a decentralised as well as secure P2P paradigm, which might boost the security of IoT as well as wireless sensor networks. Certainly, there are problems that may restrict Blockchain's application, such as the limited processing power and storage capacities of Internet of Things (IoT) devices.

PRIVACY, DATA MANAGEMENT AND BLOCKCHAIN

Both public and private centralised organisations collect massive amounts of personal and confidential data. Although the (GDPR) intends to restrict such processing, there is still much ground to cover. When used in conjunction with other effective storage systems that employ data mining methods, blockchain is viewed as a chance to improve the security and scalability of big data. Thus, there are blockchain-based privacy and security applications that may be discovered in published works. Namecoin is a public-domain blockchain project that creates a distributed domain name system. Security, resistance to censorship, efficiency, and privacy are the key advantages of a distributed DNS solution. Alexandria is a free and open-source blockchain-based media library that protects user privacy and promotes freedom of expression. Digital identification services that verify people's identities in a standardised way can improve the security and privacy of both systems. Using both hardware and software solutions, blockchain technology could be utilised to make dispersed networks more secure and reliable. By streamlining, optimising, and automating corporate procedures, Blockchain may soon become a major driver of game-changing developments in management and commerce. Many new types of online businesses are springing up as a direct result of developments in the Internet of Things and blockchain technology. Applications built on the blockchain have the potential to be used by several companies as decentralised process management solutions. Each instance of a company process could then be tracked on the blockchain, as well as SCs could handle the workflow routing, greatly simplifying and automating internal operations while cutting costs.

An undeniable strength of the blockchain is its ability to efficiently and transparently handle data. This technology's implementations and applications have not only improved data management, but also made auditability easier by default due to the verifiability of all of its processes. In this final section on blockchain-based applications, we cite studies that investigate how to best handle data in a way that is both secure and transparent.

There are various examples of cross-organizational data management in the literature, but not yet at a level to enable full interoperability across parties. Additionally, blockchain is shaking up the HR sector by simplifying recordkeeping and facilitating candidate selection. Proposes a decentralized blockchain-based approach for metadata enabling key functions and discusses implications for the long-term administration and preservation of digital archives in the context of safe distribution of data as well as management systems. If you're looking for a way to efficiently search for a predetermined set of keywords in a distributed database, look no further than Searchain, a blockchain-based keyword search system. In order to protect individuals' identities and the confidentiality of their information during data transfer, it may be necessary to implement access control as well as authentication procedures. In addition, the literature presents cloud-based decentralized as well as effective systems that employ blockchain technology. In order to facilitate the study of enormous amounts of transactions, such systems seek to address the difficulties posed by big data.

Blockchain is a good way to store and manage data in a private and secure way, but it also has some problems. Since data is kept in a public ledger, there are still some privacy and security issues to work out with blockchains. Several methods that use anonymization or encryption can be used to keep the data's privacy safe. But these processes are not a magic bullet; how well they work depends on how they are used and on the system as a whole. File sharing across networks should be done using safe methods like telehash or a content-addressed P2P file system like IPFS.

Transactional privacy is a known problem for the security of blockchain data. Enterprises and consumers are both worried about the lack of audit trails for transactions and SC activities that happen across the whole network. Also, safety measures like using a fake name are not enough to protect transactional privacy. De-anonymization techniques, for example, can be used to find out who is behind a cryptocurrency transaction by looking at its underlying graph structure. Also, it has been shown that Bitcoin transactions can show a lot of personal information.

SCs are prone to mistakes because they are like computer programmes. These mistakes can cost a lot of money. The attack on the DAO, which cost about $48 million, the problem with the Parity wallet, which allowed almost $300 million to be stolen, and the recent discovery of hundreds of vulnerable SCs are all examples of vulnerabilities that have been used recently. Since SCs are so different from other programming environments, it can be hard to understand how they work, which can make it easier to hide bad things. Ponzi schemes look like legitimate ways to invest but give their victims astronomical returns instead. On the other end of the spectrum, there are schemes that compromise private keys or don't guarantee the return of funds to investors when funding goals aren't met. We think that out of all the suggested ways to stop some of the worst SC vulnerabilities or abuses, the one that limits how expressive the underlying programming language can be has the most promise. SC checkers build a framework to make sure SCs are correct, fair, and that their weaknesses have been found. Many big, well-known companies are starting to see how blockchain technology could be useful in the IoTs.

Given what has been said, there is a lot of work to do to make sure that both the blockchain and SCs are safe. SCs could be used to fine-tune the AI systems that are widely used to control specific features or behaviours, like those of self-driving cars or drones. Smart transactions between entities and/or devices can also make real-time implementations and a lot of other things possible.

Because of this “race to collect data,” data in many areas of artificial intelligence is better and more accurate than it has ever been. Since data will be accessible through a public ledger, AI algorithms and market prediction tools will benefit from standardisation and interoperability in public blockchain systems. All of this increases what can be done with data analytics, allowing for more accurate and scalable solutions and better AI models to be used in a wider range of situations.

The open and reliable structure of blockchain could help with managing big data. But using a blockchain structure for data analytics is too much work. But in most cases, it won't be necessary to process all transactions. Instead, efficient intermediate or auxiliary structures can be built, which makes the process more efficient. So, the only solution is to take measures on the spot. Still, there are already blockchain-based solutions for storing a lot of data. Deep learning, faster computers, and more storage space have made modern auditing possible, and blockchain is already making it better. Most of the time, these methods are aimed at customers who use big data analytics to stay competitive and relevant in today's business world. AI, on the other hand, depends on machine learning algorithms, which are notoriously hard to understand. AI systems are notoriously hard to understand and explain. This is because a huge amount of data must be processed in order to train a classifier, and this number is growing much faster than a human can easily take in. The GDPR and the “right to explanation” that it sets up make it hard to explain a decision when it comes to the latter. Also, a person whose data is being used has the right to be given useful information about how any automated decision-making works. In the case of differences caused by the non-linear use of multiple parameters and the use of randomization, blockchains can provide auditable trails to show why an AI system made a certain choice. Using big data and AI makes it possible for blockchain to be used in many new and interesting ways. This may make blockchain technologies more open.

SCM AND BLOCKCHAIN

As a result of its potential to improve visibility and traceability across supply chain networks, blockchain technology could lead to more adaptable value chains. Three aspects of supply chains—visibility, optimization, and demand—stand out as particularly promising for blockchain-based applications to bring about significant advancements. Blockchain technology has many potential applications in logistics, including the detection of counterfeit goods, the reduction of paper processing, the facilitation of origin tracking, and the facilitation of direct transactions between buyers and sellers. The use of blockchain-based applications in supply chain networks has also been shown to protect data, improve IP protection, boost supply chain-wide information management, enhance customer service via advanced data analytics, and strengthen contract management mechanisms among third and fourth party logistics to address information asymmetry. Lastly, it can enhance smart transportation systems and provide new decentralized production designs, all while improving inventory or otherwise management systems across complicated supply chains (Buterin, 2015; Carboni, 2015).

CONCLUSION

There are still numerous problems to be solved, despite the widespread use of blockchain applications. Blockchains will improve in scalability, efficiency, and longevity if this is done. The properties they provide are not very novel when viewed in isolation, and the mechanics upon which they are founded have been common knowledge for quite some time. However, the fact that they possess such a wide variety of desirable qualities is what has piqued the interest of so many different sectors. Though many have proposed blockchains as a database replacement, this is not at all the case. However, previously mentioned, there are numerous situations when more conventional database systems are preferable. We also determined the specific features that were most important across all of the different use cases. This makes it easier to select the right blockchain and the accompanying procedures to adjust the blockchain to meet the precise requirements of the application.
