# Chapter 8

*   Geetika Madaan  
    Chandigarh University, Mohali, India

ABSTRACT

For data analysis and processing, blockchain is a revolutionary technology that has the potential to combine financial resources efficiently and effectively. Data, rules, and application layers are used to design new financial forms or service models based on client needs to enhance the banking system's overall efficiency and the quality of financial services. As blockchain technology automates and improves the accuracy of customer credit conditions, financial markets may be transformed. Other uses include improving the efficiency of cross-border financial operations, amongst others. In this study, the authors comprehensively scrutinized the structure of blockchain technology and its use in the financial sector. The final section of the chapter included constructive suggestions to help the development of blockchain technology in cryptocurrencies, smart contracts, initial coin offerings, the clearing and settlement of financial market transactions, and the implications of these developments for the governance of publicly traded companies.

INTRODUCTION

Large segments of the financial sector are undergoing significant transformation. Additionally, as a consequence of the financial crisis, regulatory adjustments have been implemented; nevertheless, digitization in particular has contributed to this by altering old corporate processes and allowing new rivals to join the market (Feyen et al., 2021). The majority of banking transactions are now completed on a smartphone rather than at a traditional branch, and computer algorithms are increasingly providing investing advice. High-frequency traders, or computer programmes that make and execute trading decisions in fractions of a second, dominate the world of securities trading, which is primarily computerised and controlled by them ([Musciotto et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r45)). Crowdfunding platforms coexist with established sources of corporate financing in the business world. One component of digitalization that has the potential to be significant for the financial sector is the development of blockchain technology, which began more than a decade ago. A recent report by the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) examines several emerging technologies (such as Big Data, the Internet of Things, Artificial Intelligence, and others) to assess how they impact various operations and services in the financial sector (such as payment transactions, financial investments, lending, and others) ([Casino et al., 2019](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r11)). The blockchain technology, which we shall discuss in this study, is the only new technology that has an influence on everyone's functions and services confirmed.

REVIEW OF LITERATURE

The blockchain technology is based on the cryptocurrency Bitcoin, the concept of which was published in 2008 in an article written under a pseudonym was presented. The underlying concept is to develop a decentralised payment system that is capable of operating without the involvement of “trustworthy third parties,” such as financial institutions such as central and commercial banks ([Baldominos Gómez & Mochón, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r5)). Despite the fact that cryptocurrencies have not supplanted conventional currencies, they have grown in importance dramatically in recent years.

The total market value of all cryptocurrencies is several hundred billion US dollars, and there are futures contracts and exchange-traded funds that are based on cryptocurrency investments. Smart contracts, among other things, allow for the issuance of “tokens,” which are utilised, for example, in the context of Initial Coin Offerings (ICOs), a new kind of business financing that is becoming more popular (Diaby et al., 2021). Although blockchain technology was originally developed for the purpose of creating cryptocurrencies, its application potential now stretches well beyond this. For example, it is being debated whether or not it has the potential to completely transform the processing of securities transactions. It was also predicted that the changes would have far-reaching consequences for the corporate governance of publicly traded corporations. Also mentioned have been a slew of non-financial uses, which we will not cover in this post.

But there are many who believe the impacts of blockchain technology will be more limited in scope. In order to do away with financial institutions such as central or commercial banks, the Bitcoin concept was developed (Chang et al., 2020). In reality, however, the vast majority of Bitcoin transactions currently take place on stock exchanges (and hence at financial institutions), where Bitcoin and other crypto currencies may be exchanged against one another as well as against regular fiat money, on the other hand, is manifestly in conflict with this.

Attempting to compile a comprehensive inventory of probable financial uses of blockchain technology, we provide our findings in this paper. It is organised in the following manner. First, we will outline the technical basics of the blockchain, beginning with the Bitcoin blockchain as a starting point but moving on to examine alternate design possibilities where it is appropriate (Andoni et al., 2019). After that, in the third part, we show what we believe to be the most significant financial uses of the new technology. The topics we cover most often include cryptocurrency and blockchain technology; smart contracts; initial coin offerings; securities trading; and the corporate governance of publicly traded firms. The fourth part concludes the essay by providing a forecast.

TAXONOMY OF THE BLOCKCHAIN WITH EXPLANATION

Cryptocurrencies such as bitcoin and other digital currencies are built on blockchain technology, which has been described as having “revolutionary potential” in terms of its potential influence on business models throughout the globe (see above) ([Vega & Clohessy, 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r61)). For those who are unfamiliar with the term, the blockchain may be defined as a distributed open-source database built on state-of-the-art encryption. Transactions between shady parties can be made more secure with the help of blockchain technology, which removes the need for the middleman altogether. we wouldn't have to need brokers or rating agencies or other credit agencies if that were the case, making financial transactions feasible without banks (Dutta et al., 2020). This study goes over the basics of the technology so you can see if it lives up to your hopes and expectations. An organisation and storage system, and a way to build trust in the information are the two main structural elements.

Organization and Storage of Data

Information regarding financial transactions, such as cryptocurrency applications, or information about other valuables may be stored and monitored using a blockchain, which functions as a kind of digital cash book ([Chen et al., 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r13)).

In order to fully grasp the possibilities of blockchain technology, one must first get an understanding of the cash book's fundamental elements. Because of this, we'll go through them in greater depth below. We can add records to an ever-growing list of time-stamped records in a blockchain, but those records themselves cannot be modified or deleted. Isn't it possible to keep the system up to date? Although the update is not overwritten, it is recorded as a new timestamped entry, rather than overwriting an already existing one. This means that the data structure always has a full history ([Lewis, 2015](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r39)). While it may look difficult and memory-intensive at first sight, this sort of data storage increases transparency and fraud prevention. The term “append only ledger” refers to a cash book that only enables data to be added, but does not allow data to be updated or removed. The data is organised into chunks for efficiency's sake. The addition of a new block to the system, for example, a new financial transaction, adds new data to the system. The “identifying number” of each block refers back to the preceding block, resulting in a sequential arrangement of the blocks ([Yli-Huumo et al., 2016](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r67)).

It's also important to note that a blockchain is a distributed system. There are many nodes in a blockchain instead of a single node in a traditional cash book, and each node is responsible for maintaining the integrity of the data in the blockchain. Think of these nodes as computers and you'll get the idea ([Zhang et al., 2019](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r69)). They don't trust each other. Cash books may be copied through these nodes, however, thanks to a smart technique A huge number of computers throughout the globe now have identical copies of the whole transaction history. Intuitively, this decentralised data architecture helps to avoid assaults and develop confidence in the data, which makes sense.

The Process of Data Organization and Updating a Blockchain Can Be Summarized With the Following Four Steps

Every node on the blockchain receives a copy of the newly entered transaction. In the network, but not yet in the blockchain, it is disseminated. New transactions are aggregated into one block, and the so-called consensus process (explained in more detail below) determines which node gets to send their block. The legitimacy of all transactions in this block is evaluated by other nodes. Accepted blocks are referred to in the following block. It is the outcome of this procedure that all nodes agree on the order of blocks and retain copies of the blockchain (Shrestha et al., 2020).

| Figure 1. Structure of a blockchain (excerpt with three blocks) |
| --- |
| ![Figure978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.f01](i/ch008.f01.png) |
| --- |

Promote Trust in the Data

Data structures mentioned above are only useful if they can be relied on, of course. In a decentralised system, how can trust be fostered amongst parties that are known to be untrustworthy? So how can we assure that every block in the chain is trusted by all parties? Through asymmetric cryptography and the so-called consensus process, blockchain technology provides such trust ([Kaur et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r33)).

Asymmetric Cryptography

For new transactions that are to be added to the blockchain (Step 1), asymmetric cryptography is employed to build them in such a way that their authenticity can be verified. In this case, a private and a public key are often used to create a one-way link between them. To achieve this, the sender uses her private key and any other key to encrypt her transaction.

The public key of the sender may be used to decrypt the transaction, allowing anybody on the network to confirm that it came from the sender. Ultimately, the purpose is to secure the transaction's legitimacy and integrity, allowing the sender to be identified at any moment and preventing the transaction from being tampered with ([Rutland, 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r49)). Honest nodes can only transmit transactions they can verify as valid. These kind of encryption approaches aren't exclusive to blockchain technology. Instead, they've been there for a while in the form of e-mail, online banking, and online shopping.

Consensus Mechanism

A decentralised system's transactions must be agreed upon in order to function. That is to say, how do fresh transaction blocks (see Step 2) get added to the blockchain? All that may be done is add new blocks; it's not possible to edit or remove current data. As a result, adding transactions that benefit the person who created the blockchain record is practically incentivized ([Khan et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r35)). Consensus technology, a key component of blockchain technology, enters the picture. This method controls the next node to send its block to the blockchain. On the other hand, there should be no central authority that restricts the adding of new material. A downside to a distributed system where any node may essentially provide fresh information is the possibility that dishonest parties might use such knowledge to profit themselves unfairly. Consensus-building seeks to address this very trade-off ([Sedlmeir et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r54)). To do this, you must first provide the ability to add a new block to as many nodes as possible, if not all of them. Nodes that gather new transactions are reviewed and packed in order to be able to add the next block to the current chain (the so-called miners), and a payment is provided so that a sufficient number of nodes participate in this stage. Making this rivalry relevant is vital for miners since they are in direct competition with one another. For this reason, only blocks containing genuine transactions that do not conflict with themselves or with the rest of the blockchain should be created by knots. To be included in the block candidate, no transactions that use the same asset more than once should be included (so-called double spend problem) (Yaga et al., 2018).

The Bitcoin network relies on the proof-of-work consensus process, which is the most widely used. Core to this approach is making a block more costly to create while making verifying a new block candidate's validity relatively inexpensive for everyone else. This makes “fake reports” costly and simple to identify (Idrees et al., 2021).

This is done using so-called hash functions. Bitcoin's blockchain (256 bits) uses a hash function to encapsulate a huge quantity of data (e.g., the whole block) into a tiny amount (the hash value) of fixed size ([Sobti & Geetha, 2012](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r58)). If you swap a letter or a number in the input data, the hash function will provide an entirely different result. The number of leading zeros in a hash value on the Bitcoin blockchain now defines a maximum value. In order to meet this goal, a nonce is appended to a block of data. The hash value is presently being tweaked until it fits the specifications via trial and error. The hash rate measures how many tries are made to discover a valid solution per unit of time ([Rutland, 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r49)). In order to add a block to the blockchain, the first node that comes up with a suitable solution earns a reward of 5 (see Step 2). Miners employ high-performance computers, which use a lot of energy since computational power increases the odds of success in mining. Finally, all other nodes may check that the work has been completed. It's as simple as entering the block with the nonce into the hash function, and checking whether the hash value fits the specifications (Zheng et al., 2017). It is also possible to verify the historical legitimacy of the transactions included inside a block. Any instances of a single asset being spent more than once would be flagged as double-spending (see Step 3). Honest nodes simply disregard invalid blocks, but a new valid block candidate is added to the blockchain when miners use it as a reference in their subsequent block creation (see Step 4).

Each block includes the hash value of the preceding block, forming the chain (the blockchain) from the individual blocks. An attempt at manipulation would alter the hash value of that block and so all following blocks as well, making it easy to track out who was behind the modification ([Marbouh et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r43)). A block's hash value can be thought of as its “fingerprint.”

If two fresh, accurate blocks are added to the blockchain simultaneously, there will be two different copies of the blockchain out there. In this instance, it is necessary to establish which version is the most up to date. Consensus dictates that this is the most productive chain. In most cases, this is the longest chain ([Jabbar et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r32)).

A block's inclusion in the blockchain is more assured the more blocks that follow it there are in existence. Since you have to discover a nonce first in order to use this proof-of-work technique, it artificially complicates the process of adding additional blocks. A new valid block is produced in the Bitcoin network on average every 10 minutes because of the complexity of this probabilistic trial-and-error operation. The proof-of-work technique is named after the labour done by the miner (Paik et al., 2019).

Proof-of-work ensures that everyone on the network is in agreement about where the blockchain resides and that the data it contains can be trusted. There is no “trustworthy” mediator in this situation ([Al-Rakhami & Al-Mashari, 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r3)). A successful data attack would cost an enormous amount of money and computing power, so the stakes are set so high that only honest behaviour will be tolerated. The trustworthiness of the middleman is replaced by the trustworthiness of the technology ([Xie et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r62)).

In certain cases, alternate consensus algorithms may be better suited to the blockchain's architecture. The “proof-of-stake” notion, for example, is an alternative to the resource-intensive proof-of-work process, in which the chance of being able to connect the block candidates you have developed relies on the proportion of assets that you possess on the blockchain or the values registered by it ([Lazuashvili, 2019](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r38)). In order to ensure the success of a blockchain, it is important to offer more power to people who have a stake in its success. When it comes to industrial applications, where the primary goal is not to build agreement amongst untrustworthy parties, techniques relying on a specific number of trustworthy nodes (proof-of-authority) are called into question ([Zhu et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r73)).

Types of Design

Decentralized data structures with no access constraints and possibly dishonest players are the foundation of the blockchain ideology thus far. There are many different approaches to create a blockchain. As a result, the blockchain may be used to control who has access to the information that is kept there ([Beck et al., 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r6)). In the same manner that read rights may be managed, write permissions can also be restricted. Anyone may join a public blockchain, such as the Bitcoin blockchain, and have access to the data stored there. As a result, a public blockchain provides an unprecedented level of openness ([Beck et al., 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r6)). The Bitcoin blockchain is a “public un permissioned” blockchain since anybody may essentially serve as a miner and hence make modifications to the network. Participants in industrial applications like Hyperledger or R3 corda are frequently restricted to a select group of people. A “privately-permissioned” blockchain is thus referred to ([Ernst & Young LLP, 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r22)).

It is also crucial to consider the degree of anonymity, i.e., how much personal data is needed for the implementation of the blockchain. Some cryptocurrencies, such as Monero, do not need any personal data from users (anonymous), whereas other apps, such as those that employ email addresses, must (identified). However, pseudonyms are common in the bitcoin industry ([Zarrin et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r68)).

Bitcoin and other public blockchains based on the proof-of-work principle have limited scalability and significant transaction resource consumption. Because different blockchain systems (like Bitcoin and Ethereum) are initially incompatible, direct transactions between them are not possible.

Interoperability is becoming more important, and new technologies are being developed to address this need. There are notions like “sidechains,” “state channels,” or the idea of “sharding” that may be applied to a blockchain in a variety of ways (transaction is not processed by all nodes).

AREAS OF APPLICATION WITH EXAMPLES

Consensus may be achieved between parties that do not trust each other, and without the need for a central trustworthy intermediary such as an exchange, broker, or banking institution. Many individuals prefer to buy used cars via dealerships rather than directly from the former owner, and this is a simple application example. The lack of confidence in the prior owner is a factor in this. “Transactions,” such as accidents and repairs, would no longer need faith in the prior owner if they were all kept in an immutable database like a blockchain (Uddin et al., 2021). A middleman who may be difficult to check is preferable to the auto dealer. Thus, in a situation where trust between parties is low, the new technology's competitive benefits may be utilized more effectively ([Al-Rakhami & Al-Mashari, 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r3)). Here are examples of how this technology may be used.

| Figure 2. Dimensions of payment systems according to [Berentsen and Schar (2018)](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r7) |
| --- |
| ![Figure978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.f02](i/ch008.f02.png) |
| --- |

Barter transactions employ items that many prospective trade partners are interested in, even if they are not immediately required. Only the notion that they may be exchanged at a later date or with a different trade partner (liquidity value) is enough to drive the value of such products, which are sometimes referred to as “money” ([Khan et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r35)). [Khan et al. (2021)](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r35) argues that money's primary function is to record the claims of the actors to each other. There is no need for a formal structure when working in small groups, such as when sharing favors with close friends. In vast communities, informal structures like this can no longer function. As a kind of collective memory, money serves this purpose ([Esterhuizent, 2006](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r23)). On the one hand, the ownership structure is clearly stated at all times in such a memory database. In order for the system to work properly, each monetary unit must only be spent once (double-spend problem).

Three dimensions are used by [Berentsen and Schar (2018)](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r7) to characterise currencies or generic payment systems. These are the rules that govern the creation, representation, and transfer of monetary value ([Kevin & Suresha, 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r34)) (see [Figure 2](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#f02)). A central bank, for example, may have a monopoly on money generation, or it can be done in a competitive manner, such as when looking for gold. One way to symbolise money is to put it into an account or to have it physically in the form of currency. There are two ways in which people may exchange cash: through a bank directly or as in the case of a cash exchange ([Perkins, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r47)).

The virtual representation has considerable efficiency advantages over the physical one. At the same time, the dependency on a central instance becomes negative for various reasons (lack of trust, securing the system against failure) seen. The combination of virtual representation and decentralized transfer would therefore be ideal, but was not feasible before the development of blockchain technology. However, cryptocurrencies such as Bitcoin or Ethereum combine exactly these properties, which are desirable from an economic point of view ([Reyna et al., 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r48)). The question is therefore why they have not yet been able to establish themselves on a broad basis.

This issue may be answered by considering the “triad of money,” which refers to the three primary purposes of monetary units:

*   • The function of exchanging or making a payment
*   • The role of a data store
*   • Function of computing units

When it comes to the exchange and payment functions, economies should have as few distinct currencies as feasible so that a monetary unit can perform best. Every agent must presume that a future trading partner will accept the offered money with a high degree of probability before accepting it formally ([Delahaye, 2016](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r17)). When a new currency is introduced, it is often rejected by the general public. Because one's own acceptance is reliant on the approval of other market players, it is very difficult for a new currency to go on beyond this first equilibrium. The fact that traditional currencies like the euro and dollar are legal currency in their country of origin means that merchants must accept them. Another one of the 12 reasons the time it takes to validate a transaction to its ultimate (irreversible) conclusion is also a deterrent to people using cryptocurrency instead of traditional payment methods like cash and credit cards ([Grinberg, 2011](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r29)).

Even while the value of most crypto currencies has fluctuated greatly in the last several months, this does not make them a good store of wealth or a computing unit. The protocol for most cryptocurrencies13th sets the route for money creation, so it cannot lead to a rise in the money supply via inflation, but the usage as a speculation object, the unpredictable future adaption, and the concentration of big lead are all factors to consider. Excessive price volatility may be avoided by distributing the money supply among a select few participants. There has been some reduction in volatility in the Bitcoin-USD exchange rate over the last several years, but in 2019 it was still 70.7 percent each year Comparatively, gold had a volatility of 11.5 percent per 15th of a percent during the same span of time as the volatility of the EUR-USD exchange rate (which has some similarities to cryptocurrencies in certain respects).

Stable coins were created as a response to the wide fluctuations in the value of cryptocurrencies. The essential principle is that the currency is backed by a set quantity of money. There is a good example of this in the cryptocurrency Tether, which has a USD 1 guarantee on each unit. Facebook's planned crypto currency, Libra, is also to be covered by a currency basket. 16 Due to their design, stable coins rely on a central authority, which is the company or consortium that collateralizes them (Diaby et al., 2021). That is in direct opposition to the original purpose of cryptocurrencies, which was to eliminate the need for a central authority. Due to its large volume of data generated by using a cryptocurrency, it may lead to a systemic relevance of relevant actors and a risk of monopolies, which is worsened by this (Diaby et al., 2021).

ADVANTAGES OF BLOCHIAN TECHNOLOGY

Smart Contracts

Using the term “smart contracts” refers to contracts that are automatically executed based on a set of predetermined conditions. A “smart contract” is a term used to describe a piece of code stored in the blockchain that, when certain conditions are met, is automatically executed without the involvement of the code's initial author ([Chenthara et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r14)). The use of smart contracts expands the blockchain's ability to store data in order to perform calculations. Once the code has been saved, it can no longer be altered due to the blockchain's technological nature. As a result, it gives the parties to the contract confidence that the agreed-upon occurrence will truly take place if the criteria are satisfied. Once an item has been transferred or a payment made, this is generally done immediately on a blockchain. Extreme event insurance is one such use. The claims procedure might take a lengthy time if the contract is formed in the traditional fashion with an insurance company acting as a central middleman ([Zheng et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r72)). The policyholder is concerned about the real payoff because of the complicated payout criteria and the difficult-to-understand contract terms. There are clear requirements that may be tied to real-world occurrences, such as wind speeds or earthquake intensities, that can trigger an instant payout if they are met through a smart contract's processing mechanism. Since of the blockchain's consensus mechanism, processing can be done more rapidly because the world's “actual” state can be swiftly agreed upon. Community members and, if required, other investors may arrange insurance coverage for themselves or for their loved ones. It's also possible to distribute risks more effectively by reducing entry barriers with the use of a smart contract model ([Khatoon, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r36)).

The incorporation of external information, such as weather data or securities prices, is a major difficulty when developing smart contracts on a blockchain. Oracles are the interfaces that gather and validate this kind of data and then make it available to the blockchain. In many circumstances, they offer a security concern since they rely on external factors that cannot be protected by the cryptographic methods of the blockchain (Mammadzada et al., 2020).

The Bitcoin blockchain may also be used to map simple smart contracts, such as connecting a payment to complicated conditions or the delivery of a crypto asset. As a result, the Bitcoin programming language is not Turing-complete, which means that certain sorts of computations cannot be performed using it (Mammadzada et al., 2020). It's possible to utilise Ethereum's public blockchain to execute smart contracts with increasing complexity. Distributed programmes may be developed using the associated programming language, which is Turing-complete and provides tools for standardising contracts, such as creating your own crypto-value currencies (so-called tokens).

We analyse the pros and disadvantages of smart contracts in order to determine which contracts can be mapped using this form of contract. Smart contracts are secure and transparent. Contrary to traditional types of contracts, this code is devoid of any space for interpretation since it is machine-readable. They are very secure since the terms of the contract cannot be modified and are generally encrypted. They save time and money by removing bureaucratic systems and becoming independent from external parties ([Khan et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r35)). It is important to remember that all contract information must either be stored on the blockchain or incorporated through oracles. This means that in most circumstances, a hard fork is the only way to get rid of code mistakes. To make matters worse, testing smart contracts may be tricky since they may interact with other smart contracts or other services, making it much more complex. Three percent of roughly a million Ethereum smart contracts were found to contain flaws.

This means that the efficiency of the smart contract's processing is directly connected to the contract's completeness. Smart contracts demand that all conceivable future situations in which an action is needed be documented and included in the programme code. Even if the likelihood of occurrence is very low, this is still true. So it is difficult to purposely open the process for particular instances and only achieve an agreement with the contractual partner if required ([Xu et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r64)). When the number of possible future states is enormous, and the formulation of a full contract would be prohibitively costly, partial contracts are frequent and advantageous. When it comes to employment contracts, it's impossible to specify the exact nature of the task and the terms under which the employee will be compensated using a smart contract.

Second, the usage of a shared blockchain implies that future situations, which would normally be impossible to verify for certain parties, may be tracked and utilized directly as conditions in smart contracts ([Gatteschi et al., 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r25)). It is also possible to trace the success of an end product back to the intermediate that preceded it in the supply chain. As a result, comprehensive contracts are more likely to be signed. As a result of the easy access to information, more businesses are able to enter the market ([Bottoni et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r8)).

Increased information availability, which is essential to achieving decentralized consensus, might, however, lead to additional opportunities for collusion between rivals. It's possible that, depending on the regulatory environment, this trade-off might have a beneficial or negative impact on the economy ([Abimbola et al., 2019](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r1)).

Initial Coin Offerings

It is common for an Internet firm to offer tokens, or coins of value, as part of an Initial Coin Offering (ICO). Security tokens may either confirm payment claims against the business, which may be related to the company's profit or sales (security tokens), or permit the use of the company's future services (utility tokens). Frequently, utility tokens are bought with an eye on reselling them for a profit down the road ([Toma & Cerchiello, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r59)). Utility tokens: ICOs are often a combination of the issuing of securities and the early sale of a product, as a result (see [Figure 3](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#f03)). Consequently, it is quite difficult to assess them. Existing investor and consumer protection rules and regulations do not align with the new notion, and the ambiguity of roles makes it difficult to design new legislation ([Momtaz, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r44)).

For the first time, an ICO is utilised to generate money for a blockchain-based firm. At the time of the ICO, the only thing available is a “white paper” outlining the fundamental concept and design of the product. Tokens that have just been issued are sold directly to the public, with no need for an exchange or any other kind of middleman. Smart contracts are used to conduct an ICO on a blockchain. In order for investors to avoid a direct legal link with the corporation, they should not purchase tokens directly. This year, the amount of new tokens issued by firms was USD 21.6 billion, a rise of more than thrice over 2017 and an increase by a factor of more than 80 over 2016 ([Momtaz, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r44)). Volumes fell sharply to only USD 3.3 billion in 2019. The ICOs compete with traditional venture capital finance and crowdsourcing, or they complement them in the form of hybrid models, as they have done in the past. A controversy surrounding ICOs tends to overshadow their enormous volumes, and this has led to many incidents of investors losing all of their money in these speculative investments ([Momtaz, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r44)). The SEC clearly advises against ICOs that are promoted by celebrities because of their negative reputation. The possibility of Ponzi schemes is always there since ICOs are generally constructed across several rounds and the price of tokens rises over time. Some authorities have imposed a total ban on ICOs in response to the recent incidents (e.g., China). Individual inspections and occasionally harsh rules are used to safeguard investors in most western nations, although they do not prohibit ICOs outright. Additionally, Initial Exchange Offerings (IEOs) have become a less decentralised development in reaction to the scandals, among other aspects.

| Figure 3. Classification of initial coin offerings (ICOs) |
| --- |
| ![Figure978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.f03](i/ch008.f03.png) |
| --- |

It's in this situation that a crypto exchange steps in to act as a middleman, vetting the business first and then facilitating the selling of tokens. There are two ways in which an initial coin offering (ICO) may provide value to the economy: via the creation of new businesses and the acquisition of new customers ([Lipusch, 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r41)). As a first step, they aid in the construction of platforms (cf. Li & Mann, 2018). There are numerous platform apps where the benefit of all participants rises with the quantity and activity of other users, therefore participation is only useful if there are a significant number of other participants. Investors demonstrate their commitment to the startup's platform by purchasing the (utility) tokens.

There is less of a chance that other participants' benefits may be negatively affected by fewer future participants. Thus, ICOs solve the fundamental coordination issue that ICOs were designed to address. Token price increases across many rounds of an ICO might be justified by the fact that the project has a larger value because of prior ICO commitments. The so-called wisdom-of-the-crowd effect is another way in which ICOs generate value (Salampasis et al., 2020). Investment banks aren't responsible for accumulating and assessing information in a typical Initial Public Offering (IPO).

ICOs that may be carried out across several rounds, as well as the suggestions and investment choices of these groups, allow less well-informed participants to monitor the investing behaviour of the experts and contribute to achieving critical mass. This is because ICOs are both rights of use and investments, which have a significant impact on both of these outcomes ([Zhang et al., 2019](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r69)). Because of the network effect, it is possible to see an increase in the value of tokens when there is a significant demand for them. Attention and subsequent platform adoption increase as a result of increased value ([Gong, 2009](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r26)).

Securities Settlement

On stock exchanges or over-the-counter trading platforms, securities may be purchased and sold for a profit. Once a transaction is completed, the trading process isn't done, and it continues. After determining (clearing) mutual delivery responsibilities, both parties must transmit their respective securities and purchase payment to each other (settlement). The ultimate settlement of a transaction now takes place two days following the trading day ([Adhami et al., 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r2)). Specialized institutions including brokers, clearing houses, and central securities depositories are involved (central securities depositories). Market fragmentation occurs when many systems (silos) are running at the same time, such as at Deutsche Borse, where Eurex Clearing does the clearing and Clear stream does the settlement. System fragmentation needs connections between systems in order to function properly. Complex and costly cross-system transactions are a result of this design.

Non-tangible assets may be recorded via the use of a blockchain, which establishes a consensus on the transactions that have been carried out. In this regard, a blockchain might theoretically be used to conduct transactions involving equities. Since securities transactions are subject to regulatory and tax laws, as well as privacy constraints, the blockchain requirements in this application vary from those for cryptocurrency. This precludes, for example, a wholly unidentified system design.

There are a slew of possible setups. For instance, a silo's infrastructure may be moved to a blockchain. The Australian Stock Exchange (ASX) is already well on its way to adopting this strategy. Those who are authorised participants and those who have a valid interest will have access to a private blockchain there (such as regulators). The business processes and intermediates would virtually stay the same if this solution was implemented ([Caldarelli et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r10)). If it were not feasible to design and apply common standards, the present fragmentation would continue to exist.

With the possibility of blockchain-supported securities trading, one may conceive an extreme solution that eliminates most of the middlemen, such as an entirely decentralized system in which all transactions, including clearing, are carried out on a blockchain ([Xiong et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r63)). A solution like this is discussed in detail below:

There are several benefits to fully blockchain-powered securities trading. It would be simple to trace the ownership of a security. In addition, smart contracts may be used to automate procedures such as interest and dividend payments. With comparison to the current system, processing would be substantially more efficient in this new system. Instead of the current settlement two trading days following the trading day, a settlement that is much speedier or even real-time might be implemented. Settlement risks may be reduced or eliminated if security delivery and payment were carried out simultaneously. The need for a central counterparty would be eliminated ([Schar, 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r53)).

A central counterparty accumulates settlement risks and might therefore create a “single point of failure” in the system, making this a potentially major development. A theoretical model of a public unpermissioned blockchain based on the proof-of-work method ([Yermack, 2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r66)) is developed by [Chiu and Koeppl (2019)](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r15), and it is capable of carrying out both security delivery and payment operations at the same time. The processing speed is made flexible in that market players may pay a fee, which increases the attractiveness of their transaction to the miners, resulting in the transaction being performed more quickly. The cash generated by the fees, in turn, is necessary in order to provide incentives to the miners ([Huibers, 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r30)). As a result, the capacity of the blockchain must be restricted - only in the event of blockages would market participants be prepared to pay a charge in exchange for speedier transaction processing. [Chiu and Koeppl (2019)](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r15) estimate that achieving coordination among market players on such a blockchain architecture would be challenging, and they predict that regulatory involvement may be required. Additionally, blockchain-supported stocks trading is accompanied with possible dangers and drawbacks, in addition to the previously noted difficulties in system design and compliance with regulatory, tax, and data protection standards ([Chiu & Koeppl, 2019](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r15)).

If there were just one system, the efficiency improvements would be at their maximum. As a consequence, such a system would be a monopoly, with all of the attendant issues. A move to real-time resolution would also bring forth additional issues and complications. First and foremost, the quantity of transactions that would need to be completed would skyrocket. At the present, so-called netting occurs at the close of a trading day, during which the net delivery obligations and delivery claims for each clearing participant are calculated based on the transactions of the day. Settlement is therefore limited to just those net claims that have been resolved. Real-time processing does not allow for this kind of netting, which means that each transaction must be handled “in gross,” as opposed to “net.” It is no longer feasible to initiate short sells inside a day and close them out before the end of the day because of real-time settlement. Those who work in the financial markets rely heavily on this option. They would alternatively be required to maintain large stockpiles of securities or to demonstrate that they have the capacity to deliver for real-time settlement at all times by engaging in proper securities loan transactions in the event of real-time settlement. This might have a detrimental influence on the liquidity of the stock market.

In addition, transactions using derivative financial instruments may result in extra complications. Market players are required to submit margins on a regular basis in such transactions. This collateral provides protection against losses that have already been incurred as a result of current positions as well as against future loss possibilities. Price variations result in changes in the amount of collateral that must be provided. The position of a market participant is closed forcefully if he or she is unable to fulfil an enhanced security requirement (also known as a margin call). In the case of smart contracts, these operations may be fully automated and completed in real time. This has benefits in that it reduces counterparty risk, but it also has downsides in that market players must retain liquidity on hand in case of a probable margin call at any point in time (Yaga et al., 2018). The possibility of harmful pricing consequences exists in severe instances, such as when it comes to mandatory closeouts, for example. The above reasons demonstrate that the “revolutionary” option of a fully blockchain-supported commerce is fraught with difficulties. More “evolutionary” solutions, in which just the infrastructure inside current institutions is moved to a blockchain, are expected to be adopted in the near future as a result of this ([Li et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r40)).

Corporate Governance

On the topic of corporate governance, [Yermack (2017)](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r66) explores the implications of blockchain technology. His rationale is predicated on the idea that all aspects of securities trading are supported entirely by blockchain technology. As previously indicated, this makes it simple to keep track of who owns certain securities. This makes it possible for issuers to connect with their shareholders in a timely and cost-effective manner ([Yermack, 2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r66)). By lowering the cost of voting for shareholders and increasing the function of the general meeting as a shareholder forum, suggest that blockchain technology may enhance the function of the general meeting and its role as a shareholder forum ([Lafarre & Van der Elst, 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r37)).

Because of the legal framework, as previously stated in the preceding section, blockchain-supported securities trading will not be anonymous under any circumstances. As a result, it may be presumed that at the very least the supervisory authorities are aware of the identities of the shareholders. Insider trading would be simpler to identify as a result of this, and it would be more difficult to acquire holdings in a stealthy manner while avoiding the obligations of regulatory reporting. This would allow market participants to get real-time updates on reportable securities transactions of corporate insiders (directors deals), which would limit insider trading gains while increasing the information efficiency of the market as a whole ([Sharma & Charbathia, 2015](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r55)). Re-dating of stock options for management would be made more difficult if the options were also based on a blockchain ([Singh et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r57)).

As predicted by [Yermack (2017)](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r66), less developed nations, in particular, will likely be the first to adopt blockchain-based securities trading and reap the advantages of improved governance that will follow ([Yermack, 2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r66)). It is based on the fact that current institutions in these nations are not particularly trustworthy, which raises the potential benefits of a blockchain solution when contrasted with the existing institutional structure, among other things, in his projection.

CONCLUSION AND FUTURE SCOPE

The high resource consumption and limited scalability of the major public blockchain implementations, such as those utilised by Bitcoin, are two of the most serious issues with these systems. The high resource usage is due to the incentives built into the system, as well as the proof-of-work consensus technique used to reach a consensus ([Salimitari et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r52)). It is created by the payment for each freshly mined block that an equilibrium is reached in which the miners have an incentive to bring more computer power into operation until their marginal costs are equal to their marginal revenues. An increase in the Bitcoin price implies that the marginal costs, in the form of power spending, have increased as well. As a result of this, the electricity consumption of the Bitcoin network has now reached the level of the electricity consumption of whole nations ([de Vries, 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r16)).

Secondly, there is a difficulty with scalability since the protocol specifies a maximum block size and a period between two blocks that is insufficient. Every 10 minutes, on average, a new block with a maximum size of one megabyte is added to the Bitcoin blockchain. This is known as the mining process. In practice, this means that around seven transactions may be completed each second. When compared to the needs of traditional payment systems such as Visa, the differences are many orders of magnitude greater. Solving this challenge may be accomplished via a variety of methods. Depending on your preference, you may either lower the time between two blocks (like in the cryptocurrency Litecoin, which has a 2.5-minute period between two blocks) or raise the size limit for each block (see the crypto currency Bitcoin Cash with currently 32 megabytes per block). However, all alternatives have the effect of increasing the size and, thus, the storage needs of the blockchain, which is maintained redundantly on each individual node ([Derks et al., 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r18)).

DLT designs that do not use as much resources and are more scalable are available. One such example is the cryptocurrency IOTA, which does not rely on a chain structure like that used by blockchain technology. Anyone wishing to conduct a transaction with this coin must first confirm the transactions of others. A directed acyclic graph (DAG) is used to store them, and the underlying principle is that the greater the fraction of transactions that are subsequently dependent on them, the more secure they are. The underlying consensus process, on the other hand, is far more sophisticated, and a consensus in the still-small network is now impossible to reach without the assistance of a central coordinator (Grabe et al., 2020).

In addition to desirable traits (such as the combination of decentralised transfer and virtual representation), cryptocurrency also has characteristics that are not possible to accomplish with any other known payment system. Nonetheless, we do not believe that a widespread adoption of “actual” cryptocurrencies is likely in the foreseeable future ([DeVries, 2016](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r19)). The primary reason for this is the loss of efficiency that would arise from the introduction of a second currency with a value that differs from the value of the primary currency in circulation (the legal tender). In contrast, stable coins (which include the crypto currency Libra, which was created by Facebook) do not suffer from this drawback ([Saiedi et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r50)).

But since they are backed by a portfolio of assets in conventional currencies (such as government bonds), stable coins are able to use a traditional primary currency as a benchmark, as opposed to bitcoin. Smart contracts will be the primary way in which blockchain technology will demonstrate its capabilities ([Goodell & Aste, 2019](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r27)). These enable for the creation of precise laws that are based on a broad-based agreement and that are dependent on the states that are kept in the blockchain and are therefore now visible to everyone. As a result, you increase the number of contracting options available to you. Initial coin offerings (ICOs) allow smaller investors to participate in a company's success at an extremely early stage, allowing them to benefit from their expert knowledge and encouraging others to follow in their footsteps. Another benefit is that crypto coins may be traded on cryptocurrency exchanges at a substantially higher rate than they can be traded on other early forms of participation. Platform operators may benefit from the underlying network effects because of their dual position as right of use and investment ([Brandao et al., 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch008.xhtml#r9)). As previously stated, due to regulatory constraints, the processing of securities will not be able to be transferred to an anonymous blockchain. A completely blockchain-based securities trading system does not appear to be feasible at this time, and it is unlikely to be feasible in the absence of regulatory intervention. The consequences were explored in because the corporate governance of publicly traded firms is heavily influenced by completely blockchain-based securities trading, it is unlikely that revolutionary changes will occur in the near future. On the other hand, efficiency improvements will be realised via the use of blockchain-based procedures inside the present institutional framework. Similarly, additional sectors of application not described in length in this article, such as the provision of promissory note loans, are applicable. In conclusion, it can be said that the disruptive nature of the technology, in addition to the ones already addressed, may result in totally new applications being developed, but that the creation of these applications will take longer than the evolution of current processes. Therefore, it is likely that applications will emerge even ten years after the invention of the technology, with a strong potential to profoundly alter economic life in the future, even if the technology has only been in development for a decade.
