# Chapter 9

*   Guneet Kaur  
    University of Stirling, UK
*   ArunKumar KrishnaKumar  
    Bulleiverse, UK

ABSTRACT

A new breed of consumer-facing financial applications called decentralized financial applications (DeFi) is structured using smart contracts and runs on permissionless blockchain technologies. In this chapter, the authors contextualize the DeFi idea within the theoretical framework of blockchain and related technologies and their potential real-world applications. In addition, the authors looked at the basic DeFi concepts and application scenarios for DeFi today and pinpointed distributed ledger technologies, artificial intelligence, machine learning, and zero-knowledge proofs as the three key technologies that help revolutionize DeFi. The authors concluded that blockchain is the foundation of DeFi, and AI and ML capabilities help organizations deliver crypto insights to their users to cope with the market volatility, and zero-knowledge proofs ensure the security of digital transactions in an unregulated, decentralized finance world.

INTRODUCTION

Corporate governance is currently at the forefront of banking. As the current system may be improved by more competition and less control for some ecosystems, reform and innovation are urgently needed. Several stakeholders developed alternative financial models in response to the crypto community's need for economic reform. Unquestionably, blockchain technology and cryptocurrencies have potential. An online community was inspired by this new business model, which presents chances for the financial sector. Now that a shared economy has emerged, new cryptocurrencies and well-known tokens are being made more accessible due to this new business model.

Additionally, by introducing a web-based decentralized approach that uses open-source protocols rather than conventional financial intermediaries, decentralized finance (DeFi) aims to alter the current centralized global economic infrastructure. Any situation where multiple entities control a dedicated network instead of a single central authority is referred to as decentralization. In this context, DeFi prevents using intermediaries in financial transactions (banks or any other financial institution). As a result, DeFi offers established financial institutions several chances to expand that could improve existing operations and services. However, distribution should not be confused with decentralization. Distribution focuses on teamwork amongst many systems, where many computers work together to finish a task by sharing resources and processing power. Still, it also challenges current financial services and their fundamental business model.

Therefore, institutions will play a significant role in building the ecosystem for DeFi as it is still in the early stages of innovation. By utilizing the current DeFi ecosystem and infrastructure, there are revenue opportunities, including new services and products, as well as operational efficiency that may be obtained. In addition, institutions that are able to adapt and embrace these developments will have many prospects for growth as this new financial ecosystem develops. This chapter will discuss various technologies revolutionizing DeFi, including blockchain, Hedera hashgraph, Holochain, artificial intelligence, machine learning, and Zero-knowledge proofs. To give practical touch, applications of these technologies using Ethereum blockchain, ASX's the Clearing House Electronic Subregister System, RippleNext, and Zoracles protocol as case studies are discussed. Discussion and future work followed by a conclusion section state the potential contributions by the authors and what research work can be forward in the future.

UNDERSTANDING DECENTRALIZED FINANCE

Nearly all banking, lending, and trading facets are controlled by current centralized systems run by authorities and gatekeepers. To access anything from auto loans and mortgages to trading stocks and bonds, regular consumers must interact with various financial middlemen. For instance, the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) and the Federal Reserve determine the regulations for the world of centralized financial institutions and brokerages in the United States, and Congress periodically updates the laws. Consequently, consumers have few direct access points to capital and financial services. As a result, they cannot avoid intermediaries like banks, exchangers, and lenders who profit from each financial and banking transaction by taking a cut. DeFi, also known as DeFi, opposes this centralized financial system by emancipating familiar people through peer-to-peer trades and delegitimizing mediators and gatekeepers ([Zetzsche et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r38)). Decentralized finance is made possible by critical technologies like blockchain and cryptocurrencies.

With blockchain, customers' banking transaction history is kept in a private ledger, which is owned and controlled by a sizable financial institution, when they make a transaction in your standard checking account. Blockchain is a distributed, decentralized public ledger where digital records of financial transactions are kept ([Meyer et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r24)). Distributed means that every user of a DeFi program has access to an identical copy of the general ledger, which contains an encrypted record of every transaction. Giving users anonymity, payment verification, and a description of asset ownership that is (almost) impossible to change through fraudulent conduct secures the system. On the contrary, decentralized refers to the absence of a middleman or gatekeeper in the system's management. By resolving challenging math problems and adding new blocks of transactions to the chain, parties who utilize the identical blockchain can verify and record transactions. Decentralized blockchain, according to proponents of DeFi, makes financial transactions safer and more transparent than the private, secretive systems used in centralized finance ([Zetzsche et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r38)).

In many respects, the first DeFi application was Bitcoin ([Chen & Bellavitis, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r7)). Users can truly own and control value with Bitcoin and send it anywhere in the world. This is accomplished by giving a mechanism for many people, who don't trust one another, to concur on a ledger of accounts without the necessity for a reliable middleman. Anyone can use Bitcoin, and nobody has the power to change its operating principles. The rules of Bitcoin, such as its scarcity and openness, are included in the technology ([Chen & Bellavitis, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r7)). It is not like traditional finance, where corporations can close markets and governments can generate money that devalues your wealth. Ethereum advances on Bitcoin technology ([Meyer et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r24)). Similar to Bitcoin, anybody has access to the rules, which users cannot alter. However, it also makes this digital currency programmable through smart contracts, enabling users to do more than just store and send value.

For a variety of reasons, Ethereum makes the ideal DeFi basis. Everyone has access to use DeFi since no one owns Ethereum or the smart contracts that run on it. This also implies that nobody can impose new rules on users. Behind the scenes, all DeFi products communicate using Ethereum. This indicates that a large number of the goods integrate perfectly. For example, it is possible to lend tokens on one platform, and on a different platform or application, you can trade the interest-bearing token for another. This is comparable to cash in rewards points at your bank. Ethereum, a shared ledger, has tokens and cryptocurrencies built in; keeping track of transactions and ownership is a kind of Ethereum's thing. The majority of things will be completely financial-free, thanks to Ethereum. Blockchain is one such technology that is contributing toward financial freedom. Many others like Holochain, artificial intelligence, machine learning, etc., play a crucial role in building DeFi products. The following section will throw light on these technologies in detail.

TECHNOLOGIES EMPOWERING DECENTRALIZED FINANCE

The market for digital assets and cryptocurrencies has proliferated in recent years. Institutions have started to offer custody services, facilitate cryptocurrency buying and selling, and are currently exploring methods to participate in DeFi. Individuals, venture capitalists, and digital native businesses are responsible for the current growth in DeFi. The adoption of DeFi by conventional institutions will mark a critical turning point in the industry's development and maturation. It will have an effect on the current modes of financial intermediation as well as the more extensive financial system. Here are a few technologies that will help empower decentralized finance-

Distributed Ledger Technologies

DLT stands for Distributed Ledger Technology, which is a digital mechanism for simultaneously registering virtual transactions and other comparable data in different locations ([El Ioini & Pahl, 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r12)). The key feature of distributed ledgers is that they don't have a central administration component, thanks to superior record-keeping techniques and methodologies. As a result, transactions are faster and more cost-effective because a central authority does not validate them. Instead, the network is made up of many nodes via which participants can access recorded information, reducing the network's vulnerability to cyberattacks ([El Ioini & Pahl, 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r12)).

Using encryption, DLT provides the secure and accurate storing of any information. Using “keys” and cryptographic signatures, the same can be obtained. Once the data is saved, it becomes an immutable database that is subject to the network's rules. DLT can transform the way institutions, governments, and businesses operate. For example, it can assist governments with passport issuing, tax collecting, land registry and license recording, the distribution of social security payments, and voting procedures. Finance, diamonds and other precious assets, music and entertainment, art, supply chain, and other businesses are all benefiting from the technology. Many large corporations, such as IBM and Microsoft, are experimenting with blockchain technology in addition to startups. Bitcoin, Ethereum, Hyperledger Fabric, Ripple, R3 Corda, and Quorum are some of distributed ledger technologies' most prominent use cases.

Blockchain

Blockchain (a type of DLT) is a public ledger system that ensures the integrity of transaction data ([Swan, 2015](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r32)). It is best known as the technology that powers the Bitcoin cryptocurrency, but blockchain technology has not come into existence since the introduction of the Bitcoin cryptocurrency. A study titled “New Directions in Cryptography” was published in 1976, and it introduced the concept of a distributed ledger ([Diffie & Hellman, 2019](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r10)). The foundation and development history of blockchain can be found in [Table 1](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#t1).

Table 1. History of the blockchain technology

| Year || Description |
| --- | --- |
| 1976 || Diffie and Hellman published their paper titled “New Directions in Cryptography” |
| --- | --- |
| 1977 || The first famous encryption algorithm called RSA (Rivest–Shamir–Adleman), a widely used public-key cryptosystem for secure data transmission, was proposed. |
| 1980 || Merkle Tree (a data structure) was proposed to verify the correctness of data synchronization in distributed networks. |
| 1982-1990 || General Byzantium in 1982, Elliptic curve encryption (ECC) in 1985, Paxos (a consistency algorithm with high fault tolerance) was proposed in 1990. |
| 1998-2005 || The concept of cryptocurrency was introduced in 1998, the National Security Agency (NSA) released SHA-256 in 2001, and Hal Finney proposed a reusable workload validation mechanism in 2005. |
| 2008-2009 || Satoshi Nakamoto published a whitepaper on Bitcoin in 2008 and the Bitcoin era came formally in 2009. |
| 2013 || The era of distributed ledgers plus smart contracts started with the inception of Ethereum blockchain |
| 2015-2019 || Hyperledger permissioned (private) blockchain was introduced in 2015, the first non fungible token (NFT) project, Etheria, was launched in October 2015, the idea of a decentralized automated organizations or DAOs came up in 2016, a sharp increase in initial coin offerings (ICOs) was seen in 2017, EOS (the third generation of blockchain platforms) was launched in 2018, and Facebook (now Meta) proposed Libra in 2019. |
| 2020-2021 || Blockchain’s use-cases expanding beyond cryptocurrencies, the rise of NFTs, metaverse, green blockchain initiatives, and Ethereum 2.0 are the current major trends. |

Blockchain groups transactions into blocks that are linked together in a chain and then distributed to all network nodes. When two parties want to swap a digital currency, for example, they must first initiate a transaction. Then, it is loaded into a block with other transactions that have been started. The new block is then added to the network that all of the machines in the chain have created. The network components (or miners) use mathematical techniques to check and assess the initial transaction in order to determine whether it is genuine ([Qin et al., 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r27)). This signifies that at least 51% of the computers participating agree that the transaction is correct. Another critical step is to give each block of legitimate transactions a cryptographic hash that includes a reference to the previous block in the chain. This process aids in the creation of a difficult-to-distort record chain. After the unit of value is exchanged between parties, the transaction is regarded as successful and complete.

Blockchain 1.0: Digital Currencies

Blockchain's visibility started with the birth of Bitcoin, which is still the most widely utilized application based on blockchain technology. Bitcoin is a decentralized digital currency payment system based on the blockchain whose value can be maintained without the intervention of any institution or government. The Bitcoin network's number of transfers and users is constantly growing (Kondo et al., 2014). This phase in which blockchain technology was used to develop cryptocurrencies is called blockchain 1.0. In Blockchain 1.0, Bitcoin was utilized as a digital payment system, and it's being hailed as the “internet of money's” enabler. The transactions were verified using a consensus method called proof of work (PoW). Proof-of-work was first presented in 1993 to combat spam emails and denial-of-service attacks on a network ([Keenan, 2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r16)). Satoshi Nakamoto popularized the PoW idea in 2008 to validate new blocks in the Bitcoin network ([Keenan, 2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r16)). PoW is predicated on the ability of network users to prove that a computing task has been completed. A node of processing power is used to solve a mathematical equation, and after the equation is cracked, a new block on the chain is validated. Any physical item, such as a computer that can receive, send, or forward data inside a network of other tools is referred to as a node.

Blockchain 2.0: Smart Contracts

Smart Contracts, which are tiny computer programs that “live” in the blockchain, are the new important concept. They are self-contained computer programs that run automatically under predetermined conditions, such as facilitating, verifying, or enforcing contract execution ([Zheng et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r39)). The blockchain makes it impossible to tamper with or hack smart contracts, which is a significant advantage of this technology. As a result, smart contracts lower the cost of verification, execution, arbitration, and fraud protection while allowing for clear contract definition, eliminating the moral hazard issue ([Christidis & Devetsikiotis, 2016](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r8)). The Ethereum blockchain is the most well-known in this area, with its goal of allowing smart contracts to be implemented.

Blockchain 3.0: Distributed Applications (dApps)

DApp stands for a decentralized application that does not rely on centralized infrastructure. Because it relies on decentralized storage and communication, most DApps run their backend code on a blockchain, a decentralized peer-to-peer network ([Wu et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r35)). On the other hand, a typical app has its backend code running on centralized servers. A dApp, like a standard app, can have frontend code, and calls to its backend can be made using user interfaces written in any language. However, a dApp's frontend can be housed on decentralized storage like Ethereum's Swarm.

Blockchain 4.0: Web3.0, NFTs, and Metaverse

Web 3.0 will rely on decentralized protocols, which blockchain can supply to establish an autonomous, open, and intelligent internet. Metaverse, the next great thing to experience in the next few years, is the dream project of digital titans like Microsoft, Meta (Facebook), and many others. The word “metaverse” describes a virtual place constructed utilizing 3D technology within the new Internet. This concept is strongly tied to recent technological advancements such as blockchain, augmented and mixed reality, non-fungible tokens (NFTs), etc. Non fungible tokens are a specific type of digital file representing a single unit of value in a digital world where every file is reproducible ([Wang et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r34)). NFTs function as verified assets with unique identifiers and characteristics that provide value.

Since the apps and transactions that previously required centralized systems or trusted third parties to authenticate them can now operate in a decentralized manner with the same confidence level, blockchains have caused significant disruptions to established business operations.

Hashgraph and Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG)

According to Mance Harmon (Wirdls and Hedera co-founder), Hashgraph is a first-generation technology with significant speed, fairness, cost, and security limitations ([Baird, 2016](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r1)). Hashgraph stores transactions in a DAG and employs a voting mechanism paired with the gossip protocol to obtain consensus across nodes swiftly ([Schueffel, 2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r29)). Hashgraph's breakthrough is in its consensus protocol. When compared to blockchain, it has been mathematically demonstrated to help replicate data significantly faster. A hashgraph is made up of columns and vertices at the most basic level ([Baird, 2016](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r1)). Each column represents a network user, whereas the vertices represent events. In the hashgraph, users may basically do two things:

*   • Users can submit a transaction by creating an event that contains a new transaction.
*   • Users can choose other users at random and tell them what they know about a transaction.

Hashgraphs likewise package transactions into blocks; however, unlike blockchains, all hashgraph blocks are added to the distributed ledger in the same sequence, regardless of their order or circumstances. Hashgraphs are utilized to produce a full image of the network's transactional data rather than a winner-takes-all race to confirm the blockchain data ([Schueffel, 2017](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r29)). A Directed Acyclic Graph is the name given to the resulting structure. One of the main advantages of DAGs over blockchains is that they can minimize the size of data per transaction, cut costs, increase speed, and attain higher scalability levels ([Benčić & Žarko, 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r3)). The difference between a Directed Acyclic Graph and blockchain is summarized in the [Table 2](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#t2).

Table 2. Difference between DAG and blockchain

|  || Directed Acyclic Graph || Blockchain |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Consensus achievement || The success of the previous transaction attests to the achievement of consensus. || Different consensus processes let participants create new coins. |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Transaction speed || Adding a large number of transactions with better scalability is possible because of DAGs' unique data structure. || When it comes to scalability, blockchain technology has a smaller number of transactions per second. |
| Data structure || Data is stored independently in a DAG, as a layer atop another. || In a blockchain system, verified data is stored in blocks that are linked together in an unending chain of blocks. As a result, inserting new data in each block is contingent on prior transactions, which are all encrypted. |
| Transaction validation || The success of a transaction in a DAG protocol is dependent on its ability to approve prior transactions. || The miners or validators in a blockchain system must either approve or disapprove a safe transaction. |

Hedera Hashgraph

Hedera Hashgraph is a public distributed ledger technology that uses a DAG instead of a blockchain for its architecture ([Dolenc et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r11)). A council of global business leaders, including Deutsche Telekom, Boeing, and Google, oversees the project. To obtain consensus on network data, Hedera Hashgraph employs a gossip protocol. A gossip protocol provides transaction data to network nodes, which then agree on the transactions. Nodes send all of the transaction information to other nodes at random intervals, allowing transaction information to spread quickly throughout the network. When two nodes sync—known as a “gossip sync”—each node creates an “event” to signal the end of the sync ([Krasnoselskii et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r18)). An event is a data structure containing a timestamp, transactions, two hashes of the last of each node's events, and a cryptographic signature stored in the network's memory. The history of events and accompanying hashes is referred to as “gossip about gossip,” and it takes a hashgraph, a type of DAG. Nodes can identify where a transaction should be placed in the ledger's history by gossiping about gossip within seconds.

Hedera Hashgraph's ability to validate the whole of transactions completed on its network has been called into question, maybe due to its unique approach ([Krasnoselskii et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r18)). Some opponents claim that bad actors could encourage nodes to transmit fake data due to the lack of cryptographic block confirmation methods. Also, Hedera Hashgraph has limited ability to detect and prevent such actions.

Holochain

Holochain is a distributed ledger technology that works on a peer-to-peer basis ([Zaman et al., 2022](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r37)). It differs from blockchain in that it operates differently, giving it a distinct value in the current market. Energy is used substantially in the current generation of blockchain technologies. For example, consider Bitcoin, which necessitates the use of miners to validate the blocks. To solve cryptographic puzzles, the Proof-of-Work consensus approach requires a lot of computing power, creating a tremendous energy demand, which is not suitable for mother nature ([Panwar & Bhatnagar, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r25)). The Go programming language is used in Holochain technology, while apps can be created in Lisp or JavaScript ([Zaman et al., 2022](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r37)). It is an agent-centric, energy-efficient ledger system. This means that each agent will have their own copy of the secure ledger and will be able to operate independently. It can also communicate with other network devices and provide a comprehensive service. Real-world use cases of Holochain include:

*   • Social Networks: The best use case for Holochain is a social network. It's helpful because a social network can function without being connected, and the user can save a local copy of it.
*   • P2P Systems: Holochain can be used effectively by P2P platforms. Small communities can customize it to meet their needs. The P2P platform can also interface with other networks and utilize Holochain's features.
*   • Collaborative Applications: Holochain is an excellent solution for building joint programs such as chats, debate, scheduling, and even Wikis.
*   • Supply Chains: Holochain can be highly beneficial to supply chains. Regardless of the organization, firm, or geographic location, it can provide a unique technique to manage the supply chain.
*   • Platforms for Rating: Platforms for rating can be built, controlled, and set up.

For systems that demand global consensus, blockchain is ideal. Holochain technology, on the other hand, is appropriate for systems that do not require a global agreement to function. However, the lack of a worldwide consensus has several advantages, including increased scalability, efficiency, adaptability, and extendibility.

Tempo (Radix)

Radix is another type of DLT in which transactions are stored in a distributed database. Tempo is the name given by Radix's developers to the consensus algorithm and ledger architecture (Masood & Farid, 2018). To communicate with each other, Tempo requires a network of computers connected in a peer-to-peer method. Because it's a sharded, decentralized, distributed database, it needs a way to sort events chronologically and in order of occurrence across the network. The term “database sharding” refers to the horizontal division of data in a database. The Universe is a tempo instance, and the Universe contains the nodes that participate inside it. These nodes keep a complete or partial copy of the ledger. The network's nodes work together to create a sharded, decentralized, distributed copy of the ledger with a certain level of redundancy. Clients who wish to send events such as transactions and messages across the network can connect to any network's nodes and submit their events. There are two types of events in tempo: a ledger event, which is similar to a transaction in that it alters the ledger's state, and a protocol event, which is a communication between two nodes executing a protocol sequence (Masood & Farid, 2018). Asynchronous Byzantine Fault Tolerance protects ledger events, while Asynchronous Byzantine Fault Detection protects protocol events.

When compared to blockchain networks, the Radix network has a high throughput. Only the transactions that have a conflict are validated, eliminating the need for further effort. Also, each node that participates in the validation process gets rewarded. There is no waste of resources, so a lot of computational power and resources are saved. Radix is working on a low-volatility token right now. Radix's economic model gives DLTs a massive boost since it solves the throughput problem, which is a key flaw in blockchain technology.

Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Machine Learning (ML)

Artificial intelligence (AI) and decentralized finance (DeFi) are two trending topics these days. Large and small businesses are already profiting from the growing usage of AI in the workplace. For instance, Chatbots—software applications that can converse with human users in natural language enables managers to automate customer or employee interactions, such as answering frequently requested queries or organizing appointments. Another example is prediction markets that are a great tool for predicting future occurrences using network intelligence. There was a period of optimism surrounding their possibilities in the mid-2000s, but it waned when they were met with low precision and reliability. Because of recent breakthroughs in artificial intelligence, complex artificial intelligence approaches are now employed to anticipate outcomes in addition to crowdsourcing. However, this does not rule out the possibility of artificial intelligence rendering the wisdom of the masses obsolete in the near future. It is because each business dealing in the market does its own research to make informed speculation; therefore, prediction markets are generally more accurate than general surveys or bookies' odds.

The blockchain technology that supports the crypto space brings a new dimension to the prediction market area as the crypto space continues its disruptive entrance into the financial and societal mainstream ([Kaal, 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r15)). Blockchain technology has paved the way for constructing decentralized prediction markets that a single party or operator does not control. Smart contracts are typically used to mediate wagers between different traders, with a complicated voting system determining the ultimate conclusion. Crypto prediction markets are DeFi systems that allow anyone to trade the outcome of events regardless of arbitrary criteria such as status, location, or nationality ([Brito et al., 2014](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r4)). This is achieved by using smart contracts, which, once specific criteria are met, operate autonomously and without the involvement of centralized authorities. For instance, Augur is an Ethereum-based decentralized prediction market based on the ERC-20 standard. Augur is a pioneering system in the crypto prediction market field that promises to “democratize” banking, with development beginning in 2014. Augur's initial version was released to the public in July 2018. It is a user-generated market where, for a little charge, any user can create a market for any real-world event. The event's designer determines the price of the event, which ranges from 0% to 50%, and receives money from trading fees.

Crypto trading firms like Endor and platforms like CryptoHawk.ai are taking advantage of AI's capabilities to deliver crypto insights to their users. Endor bills itself as “Google for predictive analytics.” Endor's protocol ensures that tiny traders acquire critical market data without doing their own thorough study. In addition, the firm uses data connected to the user's activities and recycles it back into their model to sharpen the model for accurate prediction. Similarly, CryptoHawk.ai is an artificial intelligence platform for Bitcoin and Ethereum traders. This platform analyses big data sets with deep learning and AI to find critical patterns, make better decisions, and make exact forecasts. Investors can make wise investments by simplifying their selections, reducing the risk of uncertainty, and taking advantage of market volatility.

Zero-Knowledge Proofs

One of the main areas of innovation and development in our digital age is cybersecurity. As blockchain technology develops, the challenge of building user confidence and preserving privacy persists. Nowadays, if someone's information has been compromised, a private key or password might not be able to identify them due to contemporary advancements in hacking. To keep the data confidential in the crypto world, an older piece of technology called Zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs) can be utilized to ensure the security of digital transactions ([Sun et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r31)). ZKP is a cryptographic approach that can be used to test whether the prover has an adequate transaction amount in the blockchain environment without disclosing any private transaction data ([Li et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r20)).

ZKPs evaluate loose and inconsequential pieces of data to mathematically demonstrate that the presence of the entire data set is highly plausible, in contrast to conventional cryptography approaches that inspect, encrypt, and send the whole piece of data ([Yang & Li, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r36)). ZKPs stop nefarious third parties from intercepting anything of value by requiring the conveyance of little, unimportant bits of information. Three requirements must be met to apply ZKPs:

*   • Completeness: The verifier must establish the existence of the entire collection of data that the prover asserts to be in possession.
*   • Soundness: Without the necessary data, the challenges that the verifier uses to verify the prover's claims cannot be finished.
*   • Zero-Knowledge: Neither the prover nor the verifier may divulge any actual private information.

Depending on the level of communication between the prover and the verifier necessary for a ZKP, one of two alternative types of protocols can be used. Interactive and non-interactive ZKPs are the two varieties of ZKPs. In interactive ZKPs, the prover must complete challenges put forward by the verifier to win the verifier's confidence in the accuracy and existence of the prover's secret information ([Martín-Fernández et al., 2016](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r22)). Until the prover satisfies the proof conditions, information is exchanged between the two parties during the verification process. Nonetheless, according to the prover's commitments, a simulated verifier will present problems for the prover to complete in non-interactive ZKPs ([Li et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r20)). Moreover, in contrast to interactive ZKPs, non-interactive ZKPs employ an automated mechanism to verify the prover's assertions rather than a human. Therefore, more software and processing power are needed for these ZKPs. In addition, non-interactive ZKPs are frequently used by cryptocurrency applications to enable users to conduct transactions without direct communication between the parties.

ZCash and ZKPs

Zcash uses zk-SNARKs (Zero-Knowledge Succinct Non-Interactive Argument of Knowledge) to demonstrate that a transaction's requirements have been met without disclosing any significant details about the addresses or values involved ([Banerjee et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r2)). Zk-SNARKs refers to a proof construction where one can demonstrate having access to certain information, such as a secret key, without disclosing that knowledge or involving the prover or verifier in any way ([Pinto, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r26)). One of the many potential uses for zk-SNARKs is the ability to create shielded transactions in Zcash. In a shielded transaction, the sender creates proof to demonstrate that:

*   • For each protected transfer, the output values add up to the input values.
*   • The sender demonstrates their possession of the input notes' private spending keys, granting them the right to spend.
*   • The private spending keys of the input notes are cryptographically linked to a signature over the entire transaction, making it impossible for a person unaware of these private keys to change the transaction.

Nonetheless, a zk-SNARK can theoretically be used to verify any relation without revealing inputs or exposing data. The function assessing whether a transaction is valid in accordance with the network's consensus rules must return the result of whether the transaction is valid or not, without disclosing any of the data used to conduct the computations, for Zcash to have zero-knowledge privacy. This is accomplished by encoding part of the consensus rules of the network in zk-SNARKs. At a high level, zk-SNARKs operate by first transforming the proposition you need to prove into an equivalent form concerning the possession of an answer to a set of algebraic equations.

Ethereum and ZK-Rollups

Zk-Rollups, which were created to accelerate transactions and lower costs, let users validate Ethereum transactions on layer 2 using zero-knowledge proofs and then broadcast the PoV (Proof of Validity) onto Layer 1 ([Bruschi et al., 2022](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r5)). The state of all transfers on Layer 2 is maintained by the ZK-rollup smart contract, and this state can only be altered with validity proof. This indicates that instead of requiring entire transaction data, ZK-rollups simply require the validity evidence. Because there is less data supplied, certifying a block with a ZK-rollup is quicker and less expensive. A ZK-rollup eliminates delays by validating the funds before moving them from Layer 2 to Layer 1, which is accepted by the ZK-rollup contract ([Garoffolo et al., 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r14)). Validity proofs, however, are computationally expensive and are not worthwhile for applications with limited on-chain activity.

APPLICATIONS OF MODERN TECHNOLOGIES IN DECENTRALIZED FINANCE

The advent of blockchain as an implementation framework has given rise to a view that most business models can be decentralized. The previous version of capital markets, banking and financial services have been quite centralized. Through the internet era the global financial markets have become quite interconnected. Yet, they remain highly centralized and rely on central bankers and regulators to maintain sanity, particularly in times of crisis. Decentralized business models on the other hand rely on the network of users or the community to act as the governance body of the system ([Kushwaha et al., 2022](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r19)). In return, the community or the members of the network receive higher compensation from the value that the business creates. Decentralization of incentives and governance go hand in hand with these business models, be it decentralized payments infrastructure, lending platform or even a social media platform.

Several of these business models are still at a very nascent stage, particularly focused on creating a balanced and scalable incentive mechanism for the stakeholders in the network. Of all these models, decentralized finance seems to have got its nose ahead in maturing as a robust example. DeFi applications can cover use cases such as economic identity, Know Your Customer (KYC), trade finance, remittance, lending, insurance, payments and more. Amongst these use cases of DeFi, some are at a more matured state than others. Despite a few hiccups through the 2021/22 macro triggered bear market, these decentralized finance systems have held up better than their centralized counterparts within the crypto industry.

A decentralized financial system works on rules that are decided by the network of participants and enforced by smart contracts. This is in contrast to traditional financial systems where the central banks set the policies and the banks follow them and serve the retail audience. This also means, any crisis situations will also be addressed by the network of participants, rather than a centralized authority. These decentralized systems enable users across the world to participate in lending, investing, payments, insurance, and several other activities. For instance, a participant from the Nordics can lend to a farm in Brazil seamlessly in small amounts. Bits know no boundaries, and DeFi operates using these principles.

Decentralized systems cannot always escape regulations from traditional finance though. The regulators can clamp down on participants in a particular jurisdiction from participating in one or more of these DeFi solutions if they want to protect the retail audience from taking undue risks. Post the Luna Terra crises in 2022 that triggered a contagion of collapses in the crypto ecosystem, the regulators will be stricter on aspects of Decentralized finance that they deem as risky ([Tjahyana, 2022](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r33)). As a result, we may end up in this hybrid world of centralized-DeFi. As much as it sounds like a paradox, it may be a good thing if the new version of financial services can draw inspiration from the incumbent system to become more robust during crisis situations. Let us now look at various DeFi examples to bring to life how these technologies are looking to change the way financial services work for generations to come.

Ethereum Blockchain: A World Computer

The Ethereum blockchain along with Bitcoin makes up more than 60% of the cryptocurrency market capitalization. While Bitcoin is seen as the store of value, Ethereum can be characterized as the new age TCP/IP. Yet, unlike TCP/IP, one can invest into the protocol layer in web3 and monetize the growth. Over the years, Ethereum has acted as the bedrock for several emerging web3 use cases like decentralized finance, non-fungible tokens, gaming, social media, and even the metaverse ([Ferretti & D'Angelo, 2020](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r13)). Some of these application verticals have a market cap of several billion dollars that shows the maturity that Ethereum has achieved as a protocol layer in the last few years. Of all these, the DeFi vertical is perhaps the most mature.

The first wave of DeFi largely relied on the Ethereum blockchain as applications such as Uniswap and Compound started to scale operations. The health of the ecosystem is represented by the metric transaction volume locked (TVL) ([Stepanova & Eriņš, 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r30)). This identifies the sum of money that the DeFi platforms have processed. The TVLs on Ethereum based DeFi applications skyrocketed through 2021. At its peak in November 2021, the TVL was close to $160 Billion. This growth was despite the high gas fees that Ethereum network was charging. Through this period several other layer 1 protocols emerged as Ethereum competitors. Notable ones were Solana, Avalanche and Near.

Despite the competition, Ethereum has the first mover advantage and the network effect that the community was able to bring about. With a developer base that the network had built over the years, there were new DeFi applications that were getting launched and funded by institutional VCs. The Ethereum Virtual Machine (EVM) environment acted as a key catalyst for most developers to build on the chain ([Ma et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r21)). Competition had to create compatibility with EVM to ensure they didn't miss out on the user base that Ethereum commanded. Some of the key names within the Ethereum DeFI ecosystem are as follows,

*   • Uniswap for decentralized crypto exchange;
*   • Lido for liquid staking;
*   • Aave for lending;
*   • Yearn Finance for yield aggregation;
*   • DyDX for derivatives; and
*   • Armor for insurance.

This is by no means an exhaustive list of Ethereum based DeFi applications. Each of these applications individually managed several millions to billions of dollars in TVL in Q4 2021. Many of these applications are also backed by smart money as investors like A16Z have deployed billions into them. As this application layer scaled its user base, the Ethereum blockchain benefitted from the network effect. Other blockchains lacked the maturity of the underlying infrastructure and as a result the seamlessness to create and scale applications on their blockchain.

Ethereum’s migration from Proof of Work consensus mechanism to Proof of Stake that happened in September 2022 should please ESG investors as the chain’s energy footprint is going to go down drastically. Many institutional investors who haven’t been able to invest in blue chip cryptos like Bitcoin and Ethereum due to ESG principles, will no longer have those constraints. Apart from the investment angle, the merge will allow Ethereum to become more deflationary. The economic model is also getting upgraded with a view to incentivise users that perform certain transactions. This is analogous to credit card users getting points or avios that can be used elsewhere. This is expected to increase the usage of the chain even more.

Therefore, Ethereum is a blockchain that is decentralized and is ESG friendly, with a developer friendly ecosystem based on EVM, and an ever-increasing suite of applications that interact with each other. Despite all these components coming together, Ethereum is not without its challenges. The upcoming merge is not going to address the high gas fees problem that was particularly bad in 2021. As other chains like Solana and Avalanche mature as an ecosystem, developers and users will start embracing these platforms due to very low transaction costs and high transaction throughput. The team at Ethereum are working on sharding to address these infrastructure issues, but they seem to be medium term solutions. Yet, needless to say that Ethereum has demonstrated its ability to be the network layer of the future. It shows promise and has challenges which are being addressed by the ecosystem. As the network grows, Ethereum could soon be the crypto with the largest market capitalization, and flip Bitcoin through the next few years on market capitalization. It is truly the technology for the next generation.

Digital Asset and ASX Collaboration on CHESS Replacement

In 2016, the Australian Securities Exchange (ASX) announced that its post trade platform will be built on Blockchain. The post trade platform was called the Clearing House Electronic Subregister System (CHESS) platform and it was planned to be the first industrial application for blockchain within financial services (Chen, 2016). The blockchain firm digital asset worked with ASX to build CHESS. Previous to agreeing to the blockchain implementation, the CHESS system existed as the core clearing and settlement platform for the Australian cash equity market. However, CHESS was based on legacy technologies and was slowly coming to the end of its lifespan. This coincided with the rise of Blockchain as a key technology paradigm. The board and the technology leadership at ASX wanted to ensure that the next version of CHESS is built using a fundamentally superior and futuristic technology. While the decision to use blockchain for CHESS in an institutional setting is considered a huge risk, the technology already seems to have reduced the complexity in the system.

For instance, the legacy CHESS system used 522 messaging constructs (Chen, 2016). The new system is reducing that to 90 messages through a process of mapping and consolidation. The first roll out will be a private permissioned blockchain behind the ASX firewall. The first prototype of the solution was released in September 2017, however the roll out of the live application has had several delays since. The system is now going through several iterations of testing, with the functional specifications and expectations clearly defined in the project documentation. The timeline set for the live roll out of the CHESS platform is April 2023, but even that is now anticipated to see further delays. Despite these hiccups as this technology and industrial implementations using this technology improves and matures, we should see more seamless executions.

RippleNet: Global Payment Network for Financial Institutions

One of the key use cases of blockchain technology is financial transactions and payments. With the advent of Fintech, payments within national borders have become mostly frictionless and seamless. However, international payments and remittances still remain complicated for both retail and business transactions. Ripple is at its heart, a payments network. Founded in 2004 by Ryan Fugger, Ripple gained its web3 identity in 2012 when Chris Larson, David Schwartz and their team created the XRP ledger ([Reed-Woodard, 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r28)). The traditional financial services industry uses Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunications (SWIFT) payments. This is nothing but a messaging system that's used by banks to process transactions. While SWIFT has been quite effective all these years, the multiple hops that money has to talk through the correspondent banking system makes it quite a blackbox.

Ripple has targeted this institutional payment use case and have been able to create a product that has better throughput than the Bitcoin Blockchain. Thanks to the XRP ledger consensus protocol that is neither proof of work, nor proof of stake, this protocol offers block creation generally under five seconds. Banks that use the Ripple network can act as validators of the ledger to ensure consensus is reached on transactions. Apart from the throughput achieved, Ripple also offers On-Demand Liquidity (ODL) which allows for counterparties to execute transactions without having to pre-fund the transaction. This is particularly useful in cross border and cross currency transactions. With an architecture that offers meaningful throughputs, and removes operational overheads within the remittance space, Ripple’s commercial growth seems promising.

While Ripple’s execution capabilities seem top notch, there are broader ecosystem challenges that can act as headwinds to its growth. Ripple is reliant on working with the banking infrastructure to enable the remittance feature across borders. But banking infrastructure and operational processes are highly inefficient, and any meaningful scale can only be achieved through a number of banks participating in the Ripple infrastructure. Apart from these operational headwinds, Ripple has also been affected by regulatory headwinds. The Securities and Exchange Commission sued Ripple in 2021 claiming that Ripple raised $1.3 Billion in digital assets that weren't registered as security. This lawsuit had a serious impact on the XRP token. Many in the crypto ecosystem feel this is just the beginning of such attacks by the regulators on major crypto companies. Only time will tell how Ripple will shrug off its challenges, and achieve scale globally.

Zoracles Protocol: Zero-Knowledge Proofs Oracle

The traditional banking industry has been reasonably efficient over several decades despite several market cycles. But financial services across the world are interconnected as we saw during the 2008 crisis. Yet the key macro decisions within a country are often very centralized and affect all citizens who are part of the banking system. With the advent of Blockchain technology and DeFi, the constraints of national borders and regulatory bodies are largely irrelevant. Therefore, this is a subcluster of blockchain use cases that is expected to mature faster than most others. In the bitcoin cycle that ended in 2018, we saw the rise of several crypto exchanges, but most of them were centralized. But in the previous cycle that peaked in 2021, we have seen several DeFi models and economies emerge.

One of them is the Zoracles protocol. Zora labs leads the efforts behind this protocol began their journey with the mission to provide zero knowledge oracles for smart contracts that DeFi runs on. Two key data elements that they focus on are price data, and credit history information. Before we understand these use cases better, let us briefly look at what ‘zero knowledge’ within the cryptographic space is. Zero knowledge proof is a cryptographic technique that allows a specific piece of information to be validated without having to reveal the information ([Couteau et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch009.xhtml#r9)). For instance, party A might want to prove to party B that they have the password to a system without letting party B know what the password is. This is particularly useful when we want data privacy, yet can share and transmit data seamlessly.

Zero knowledge oracles help deal with confidential data across the DeFi landscape. Zoracles protocol has been developed with precisely that goal. The key use case for Zoracles is consumer credit history. Therefore, if there is a DeFi user looking to borrow on the platform, Zoracles provides the credit data to the smart contract with high levels of confidentiality. As DeFi use cases scale across products like car loans, mortgages, or any other form of collateralized lending Zoracles protocol can be a critical component in the underwriting process. It can perform credit score checks with credit bureaus and pass on the information to the DeFi application that will then be able to make the decision. This process would be performed confidentially and securely to verify the identity of the consumer. With all these different use cases of blockchain, particularly in decentralized finance, the future definitely looks promising. Risks remain and the ecosystem is still learning from key structural and systemic issues. But in time, with robust risk management practices, informed by secure and high-quality data, DeFi should soon become a household term.

DISCUSSION AND FUTURE WORK

An open-source movement led by technologies like blockchain, AI, ML, etc., is called DeFi. Everyone is welcome to examine, fork, and innovate on the DeFi protocols and applications. Because of this layered stack (all protocols share the same assets and primary blockchain), combining different protocols to create new combo opportunities is possible. In this chapter, the authors have looked at the basics of DeFi, technologies revolutionizing DeFi, and potential applications linked to the rise of consumer-facing DeFi protocols. Although DeFi applications running on permissionless blockchains have the potential to alter consumer-facing financial services, there are still significant risks (such as failure due to code errors) involved with using these applications. Therefore, before committing or allocating funds to DeFi apps, future stakeholders thinking about engaging with these applications should analyze and assess the significant risks.

The chapter has discovered that the Ethereum blockchain is the foundation of decentralized finance while Bitcoin is the first use case of DeFi. From remittances to securities clearing and settlement, DeFi has the potential to improve financial transactions and payments. For future work, researchers interested in DeFi apps might approach the subject matter in various ways, building on earlier research on the market structure of DeFi applications or problems with governance tokens, among other things.

CONCLUSION

Decentralized financial applications, or “DeFi,” are a new class of open financial apps that are built on permissionless blockchains that are available to everyone. Although academics in the information systems and management fields acknowledge blockchain technologies' novelty and potential influence, there is still little theoretical or empirical research on DeFi. 'DeFi' is conceptually introduced in this chapter by the authors within the theoretical framework of blockchain and related technologies and their potential real-world applications. The researchers observed that blockchain, though widely recognized, is not the only technology that helps build a decentralized financial system. For instance, because the government will tighten Defi restrictions, Zero-Knowledge Proofs are more appealing. Regulators want DeFi to become regulated because they view it as the Disneyland of the financial sector. Through the use of Zero-Knowledge Proofs technology, blockchain-based assets offer a method to make their presence viable for all users.

The authors concluded that blockchain serves as the cornerstone of DeFi, AI and ML capabilities assist businesses in providing users with cryptocurrency insights to help them deal with market volatility, and Zero-knowledge proofs guarantee the security of digital transactions in a world of unrestrained, decentralized finance. However, future researchers can work on the market structure of DeFi applications or problems within the DeFi sector, such as with governance tokens, among other things, to complement the current research work.

ABSTRACT

To encourage industry discussion of cutting-edge blockchain applications, this chapter was written. This chapter provides an introduction to blockchain technology as well as details on how to secure transactions using technology and how the secure element works. It examines common implementation worries and provides use cases for current commercial or trial projects. The following paragraphs go over the concept of a blockchain, how bitcoin transactions operate, and how to finish a transaction. The network of Marco Polo will be highlighted. In this chapter, the authors show how to use blockchain and cloud technology with Marco Polo to remove corporate pain problems. The chapter goes on to give a full overview of blockchain, including its function, traits, and applications.

INTRODUCTION

Providing Blockchain Technology as a Service Distributed ledger technology (Bitcoin) platforms are offered by third-party suppliers so that companies may create their own blockchain-based applications. It's possible to conduct many kinds of transactions and checks using blockchain-based applications. Thus, programmers that handle financial transactions, hold identities, and coordinate supply chains are among of the most common blockchain uses. Even Nevertheless, there are several scenarios in which a blockchain-based programmer may be valuable ([Haleem et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch010.xhtml#r19)).

Most companies choose to work with blockchain as a service providers rather than build their own blockchain infrastructure so they can run enterprise-level apps. Subscription-based cloud platforms allow customers to build applications without investing capital into servers, complex infrastructure, or dedicated IT staff. Firms offering blockchain as a service are responsible for managing the underlying hardware ([Haleem et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch010.xhtml#r19)).

According to [Haleem et al., (2021)](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch010.xhtml#r19) that there are numerous parallels between blockchain platforms and blockchain-based solutions provided as a service. Several businesses now provide their own proprietary platforms, often in addition to a hosted “as a service” option, to better serve their customers. Contrarily, maintaining a traditional platform is substantially more work than maintaining a cloud-hosted software service ([Haleem et al., 2021](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch010.xhtml#r19)).

To be included in the Blockchain as a Service Providers group, an application must satisfy the following requirements:

*   • Built on top of a blockchain or other distributed ledger system.
*   • Provide access to a hosted, shared database that supports transactions.
*   • They should provide a development setting and/or premade tools to make it easier for others to create blockchain-based applications.
*   • Provide ways for users to make the app their own and add new features.

There has been discussion over the use of private block chains in commercial settings. Computerworld referred to the selling of such privatized block networks without a good security architecture as “snake oil” ([Gökalp et al., 2018](#978-1-6684-7808-0.ch010.xhtml#r16)).
